# The Oby runner API

The runner is Oby's engine without the Mac app's window: the brain and the Lead, Oby's own clones and job worktrees, the Claude Code and Codex runners, accounts, routing and Publish. It runs on an always-on machine (first: `tetrix-lab`, Ubuntu, on Wellington's tailnet), so the iPhone app (and later the Watch) can say what to do, and follow the jobs, while the Mac is closed.

This document is the contract between the runner and its clients. Change it in the same pull request as the code on either side.

The Rust daemon `obid` (`core/`) serves this same API, so the apps run against it unchanged, plus the endpoints in [obid only](#obid-only) at the end; the Swift runner is frozen and never implements those. **`obid` never merges**: everything below where the runner merges a pull request (the Mac check's "Passed" merge, its conflict rounds and its recovery's retries, the `merge` inbox item) is the Swift runner's only. [obid only](#obid-only), "What obid leaves out", says what obid answers in those places instead.

## Reaching it

## LAN gateway (#284)

A Mac runner (below) keeps its API on loopback and starts one more listener, the gateway, on every interface at `:8810` (`OBY_GATEWAY_PORT`), advertised over Bonjour as `_oby._tcp` under the Mac's name so the iPhone finds it without an address. A tailnet runner has `tailscale serve` instead and binds nothing else. On the local network the gateway is plain HTTP plus a device token; away from home the Mac's tunnel carries the same requests.

- **Pairing.** The Mac app makes an invitation on the loopback API, with the runner's own token: `POST /v1/pairing/invite` with `{"url": "https://oby-….tetrix.pro", "name": "<the Mac's name>", "hostname": "oby-….tetrix.pro"}` answers `{"url", "name", "hostname", "link", "secret", "code", "expiresAt"}`: a high-entropy one-time `secret` (in the QR code, with the `url` and the `link`), a six-digit `code` to type instead, both good for two minutes and once; five wrong tries end the invitation. These endpoints exist on a Mac runner only and are never reachable through the gateway: nothing that reaches the gateway can mint an invitation, and no file on the Mac stands for the app. Settings › Runner › Pair a device advertises a route the phone can reach over HTTPS: Remote access's hostname while it's on, else the gateway as `tailscale serve` publishes it on the tailnet (the app runs only `tailscale serve status --json`, from `/usr/local/bin/tailscale`, `/opt/homebrew/bin/tailscale` or `/Applications/Tailscale.app/Contents/MacOS/Tailscale` (with `TAILSCALE_BE_CLI=1`, so the app's binary acts as the CLI), its output capped at 1 MiB, and takes the `host:port` whose `/` proxies, or whose TLS-ending TCP forward goes, to `127.0.0.1:<the gateway's port>`; when any Funnel `host:port`, in the background config or a foreground session's, reaches the gateway on any path, there's no tailnet route and the screen says to turn Funnel off first), then `{"url": "https://mac.tail0000.ts.net:8443", "hostname": "mac.tail0000.ts.net:8443"}`. With both, Remote access is chosen and the user can pick the tailnet instead; the screen says which route the QR uses. With no `gateway` in obid's configuration, neither works, and it says so. With neither, it says to turn on Remote access or run `tailscale serve --bg --https=8443 http://127.0.0.1:8810`.
- **Redeeming.** The phone sends `POST /v1/pairing` to the gateway with `{"credential": "<the secret or the code>", "name": "<the phone's name>"}` and gets `{"token": "<device token>", "deviceID": "…", "expiresAt": "…", "link": {"name": "<the Mac's name>", "hostname": "oby-….tetrix.pro"}}`. The token is shown once: the runner keeps only its SHA-256 digest, with the device's name, in `devices.json`, and the phone keeps it in its keychain. Tokens last 30 days. The `link` lets a phone that typed the code link the Mac as the QR code would.
- **Paired devices.** `GET /v1/pairing/devices` on the loopback API lists them, `[{"id", "name", "created", "expiresAt"}]`, never a token or digest; `DELETE /v1/pairing/devices/{id}` revokes one (`204`), and the phone must pair again. Settings › Runner › Paired devices on the Mac shows this list with Revoke.
- **Every other gateway request** sends `Authorization: Bearer <device token>`. The gateway checks the method and path against an explicit allowlist (Runner/Routes.swift: exactly the routes the iPhone uses, and the Watch through it) before forwarding to the loopback API with the runner's own token. A route not on the list is `404` whatever the token; a missing, wrong, expired or revoked token is `401`. Adding a route to the loopback API doesn't open it through the gateway: that's a change to the list, on purpose. Off the list: `/v1/checks/…` and `/v1/agent/…` (the Mac's own and an agent's own calls, over loopback), `/v1/connections…` (managed on the Mac), `/v1/speech` (the Watch's own API has it), `PUT /v1/settings/jobs` and the pairing endpoints.
- **Scrubbing.** Before an answer or an event leaves the Mac, the gateway nulls every `session` (an agent's session id, which resumes its conversation), replaces the accounts' home paths with `[home]` in every string, and runs the vault's redaction over the text (`[vault: name]`), the same that cleans the runner's logs. Event streams are scrubbed a frame at a time, so a secret split across chunks never goes out in pieces.

- **Base URL:** `https://tetrix-lab.tail8de49b.ts.net:8443` (the app lets you change it), or `http://127.0.0.1:7777` for the runner on the Mac itself (below). Everything is under `/v1`.
- The runner listens on `127.0.0.1:7777` only. `tailscale serve --https=8443` publishes it on the tailnet with a real HTTPS certificate, so iOS needs no exceptions, and nothing is reachable from the internet. Port 443 on tetrix-lab belongs to the Tetrix stack's own proxy, so Oby uses 8443, one of the ports Tailscale serves HTTPS on.
- **Who you are:** `tailscale serve` adds `Tailscale-User-Login` to every request. The runner answers only its owner's login (set when it's installed) and returns `401` to anyone else. The app sends no token and stores no secret for this. The one exception is `/v1/agent/…`, which agents call from their sandbox on the runner itself (`oby-check apple`, Apple check below): those requests carry their job's token instead, `Authorization: Bearer <token>`, and answer nothing else.
- **On a Mac** (#283): the same runner runs on the user's own Mac, as the Oby app's login item (`Contents/Helpers/oby-runner` in Oby.app, registered from `Contents/Library/LaunchAgents/com.deskree.oby.runner.plist`; Settings › Runner › "This Mac"). It's a Mac runner when `OBY_OWNER` is unset (on Linux that's an error), and then nothing publishes it: the base URL is `http://127.0.0.1:7777`, and there's no login header to read.
  - **The local token.** The first time it starts, the runner makes a token of its own, 64 hex characters, and keeps it in the macOS keychain; `OBY_HOME` defaults to `~/Library/Application Support/Oby/Runner` on a Mac, inside the app's own folder but never the same (each has its own `oby.sqlite`, clones and worktrees). The Oby app on the same Mac reads that keychain item and sends the token on every request, `Authorization: Bearer <token>`. Anything else is `401` (`This runner answers only Oby on this Mac.`). `/v1/agent/…` carries its job's token, as above. The token never leaves the Mac: it's not in any answer, event or log.
  - **Loopback only.** A Mac runner binds `127.0.0.1` and, before anything else, refuses with `403` (`This runner answers only on 127.0.0.1.`) every request whose `Host` isn't `127.0.0.1`, `localhost` or `[::1]`, with or without a port: a web page the user opens can't have their browser reach it through a name that resolves to 127.0.0.1 (DNS rebinding), and a request with no `Host` isn't let in either. Remote devices reach a Mac runner only through the gateway (F18), never directly.
  - A Mac runner doesn't update itself (Sparkle updates the app, #282) and runs agents in their own sandboxes rather than bubblewrap (`Agents.sandboxed`). Everything else in this document holds. Its keychain items are its own (`com.deskree.oby.runner.vault`), apart from the app's.
- **JSON** everywhere, camelCase keys, dates as ISO 8601 strings (`2026-10-09T21:43:30Z`). Errors are `{"error": "One line a person can read."}` with a 4xx or 5xx status.
- **No vault secret** in an answer or an event: every string has the vault's secrets, plain or encoded, taken out. When the runner can't read its vault, answers are `503` with the reason, and events aren't sent.
- **The Apple Watch** can't run Tailscale, so without its iPhone it uses a second, smaller API through a Cloudflare Tunnel: [The Watch API](#the-watch-api), below.

## Objects

### Job

```json
{
  "id": "9f08fd10",
  "request": "Review oby PR #33",
  "agent": "claude",
  "account": "default",
  "repo": "deskree-inc/oby",
  "branch": "oby/review-33",
  "base": "dev",
  "pr": 33,
  "kind": "review",
  "task": "Review pull request #33 in deskree-inc/oby…",
  "status": "running",
  "step": "Running pnpm test",
  "summary": "",
  "session": "e6a24229-3d3b-417b-a610-691c636b4c49",
  "created": "2026-10-09T21:42:53Z",
  "started": "2026-10-09T21:42:55Z",
  "finished": null,
  "commits": null,
  "published": null,
  "pushed": null,
  "publishError": null,
  "summaries": null,
  "issue": null,
  "fromPR": null,
  "rounds": 1,
  "round": 1,
  "check": null,
  "model": null,
  "complexity": "complex",
  "effort": "high",
  "notes": null,
  "pullRequest": null,
  "pullRequestError": null,
  "dismissed": null,
  "reviewing": null,
  "review": null,
  "reviewError": null,
  "reviewOf": null,
  "files": null,
  "lead": null,
  "claim": null,
  "limit": null,
  "stalled": null,
  "startCommit": null,
  "firstSwiftBuildSeconds": null,
  "outgoing": [],
  "moves": [],
  "question": null,
  "mode": "bypass",
  "permission": null,
  "macCheck": null,
  "mergeError": null,
  "appleCheck": null,
  "appleRan": null,
  "tetrix": null,
  "startWhen": null,
  "waiting": null,
  "mergeNeedsYou": false,
  "reviewNeedsYou": false,
  "title": "Review oby #33",
  "pipeline": {
    "number": 2,
    "total": 5,
    "stages": [
      {"id": "prepare", "title": "Prepare", "state": "done", "detail": "Clone and worktree"},
      {"id": "work", "title": "Work", "state": "current", "detail": "Round 1 of 1 · Running pnpm test"},
      {"id": "publish", "title": "Publish", "state": "upcoming", "detail": "Secret scan, push and pull request"},
      {"id": "review", "title": "Review", "state": "upcoming", "detail": "Waiting for review"},
      {"id": "merged", "title": "Merged into dev", "state": "upcoming"}
    ]
  }
}
```

- `agent`: `claude`, `codex` or `cursor`. `account`: an account `id`. `kind`: `review`, `fix`, `build`, `test`, `investigate` or `research`.
- `status`: `queued`, `running`, `done`, `failed`, `stopped` or `interrupted`.
- **On obid, few jobs fail** (the owner's decision of 2026-10-18). Like Claude Code, which ends only at a usage, turn or budget limit, a crash or a stop, and whose "success" means only that its model stopped, obid keeps `failed` for dead ends: nothing ran (the job sandbox, the repository, its clone, its worktree or branch, an attachment, its account couldn't be set up), or the agent stopped before it had a session to pick up. A job that stopped part way with a session to pick up **needs you**: it is `interrupted`, its commits kept on its branch and nothing pushed, `reason` says why and `nextStep` its one next step. That's a check that still fails after the last round (`One more round`, with `limit` set), a stall (#773: `Try again`, with `limit` and `stalled` set, the handoff note in `stalled.note`), the agent stopping mid-round (a usage limit, a login, a crash: `Try again`, after the reset or the sign-in), the secret scan still blocking after its fix round (`Try again` when every finding is one a round can fix, else none: a person looks at the branch), and a round whose summary starts `FAILS AS ASKED` (none: nothing publishes a check that fails, by hand neither; tell it what you want). Both next steps send the Try-again words (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`), which pick the session up again and clear `reason`, `nextStep` and `limit`. `summary` stays the agent's own last summary (`reason` when it said none), so it never hides what the agent did; on a `failed` job it is the error, as before.
- `reason` (obid only): why the job stopped without ending done, in obid's words, or, on a `done` job, what it didn't do (`Nothing committed, so nothing was published.`, `A review made commits; nothing was published.`). Null while it runs and once it ends done otherwise. `nextStep` (obid only): on an `interrupted` job, `One more round` or `Try again`, what its one button says; null otherwise, and on an `interrupted` job only your words or Dismiss take on.
- `resumesAt` (obid only): when obid tries an `interrupted` job again by itself, or null. A job its account's usage limit stopped, whose tool said when the limit resets (Claude Code's `rate_limit_event` with a window at 100% or `status` `rejected`, Codex's `account/rateLimits/updated` with a window at 100%, or failing those the tool's words: `…limit reached|<epoch>`, `in 2 hours 15 minutes`), gets `resumesAt` a minute after that reset, and its `reason` ends `Oby tries again by itself after the reset.` (Sangam's "Wait for the reset"). obid looks every 15 seconds and, once it has come, sends the Try-again words itself, as `nextStep` would; it's kept with the job, so a restart keeps waiting, and a Mac that slept through it tries on waking. Try again (or One more round) before then, Dismiss, or a follow-up of the job, and obid doesn't try it by itself; a reset already a minute gone by when the job stops arms nothing (a stale one would loop), and a clock time in the tool's words ("at 10:45 PM") isn't read, since obid doesn't know the Mac's zone: then it's Try again by hand. The apps say when under the reason: `At 3:04 PM, or now with Try again.`
- `step`: what it's doing now, while queued or running. `summary`: the agent's final message; its first line is the one-line notice.
- A `build`, `fix` or `test` job that ends with no commits of its own (on obid, none to publish: a follow-up counts the first job's it carries, below) is `failed`, and its `summary` starts with `Nothing committed: ` followed by the agent's first line; on obid it's `done`, with `commits` 0, `reason` `Nothing committed, so nothing was published.` and the agent's summary saying why. Not a round the user started with Try again (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages` with the Try-again words): the user decides what it should do (reset the branch, drop the work), so that round ending with nothing committed is `done`.
- On obid, the prompt of a `build`, `fix` or `test` job tells its agent to commit each small step that works as it goes (Conventional Commits, unsigned), and always before a turn ends. A job (not a `review`) that ends without commits to publish (`stopped`, `interrupted`, `failed`, or `done` with none of its own) and leaves uncommitted changes in its worktree gets one commit of all of them from obid, `wip: work left uncommitted when the job ended`, made in the job's sandbox and kept on its branch (#606). It is never published by itself and never counts in `commits`: the job's next run (Try again, One more round) takes it back out, so its agent finds that work uncommitted, as it left it. A shutdown keeps nothing: the job carries on after the restart. Work left uncommitted beside commits of its own stays in the worktree.
- On obid, a `review` job that ends with commits of its own is `done`, its review standing in `summary`, `reason` `A review made commits; nothing was published.`, and `commits` counts them; they stay on its branch and nothing is published (#630).
- When the job's account hits its usage limit mid-round (Codex: "You've hit your usage limit… try again at 10:45 PM"; Claude Code: a `rate_limit_event` at 100%, or a usage-limit result), the runner marks the account full until the reset time it named (an hour when it named none), and the job does that round over on the next account with room, the same tool first, then another tool, in the same worktree and branch with a new session: `account`, `agent` and `session` change, and `step` says so. Its `summary` then ends with a line like `Codex · Deskree hit its limit (resets 10:45 PM); continued on Claude Code · Personal.` When no account has room, the job is `failed`, and its `summary` says when the first one resets. An account marked full isn't picked for a new job before its reset (`fullUntil`).
- `base`: the branch the work started from (filled in once the job is ready). `pr`: the pull request it works on, if any. `fromPR`: on a job about an `issue` that picks up an existing pull request ("finish #19 from PR #124"), that pull request: the job's branch starts from its head, `base` stays the branch it would have had (`dev`), only the commits after that head count as the job's, and the job publishes a pull request of its own. Null otherwise; older runners don't send this key.
- `commits`: commits made beyond `base` (set when a job ends as done). On obid, a job that ends any other way (`failed`, at a `limit`, `stopped`) with commits of its own counts them too, as they're kept on its branch (obid's `wip:` commit never counts), so its follow-up starts from them and Dismiss warns (#757).
- `diff` (obid only, #736): how big the job's change is, `{"files": 2, "added": 41, "removed": 11, "byFile": [{"path": "src/jobs.rs", "added": 34, "removed": 9}], "round": 2, "rounds": [...]}`: `git diff --numstat` of its worktree against `startCommit` after each round's turn that ends well (a turn that fails or is stopped records nothing for its round), read in the job's sandbox with no rename detection, text conversion or external diff. Paths and counts only, never what a file holds; a path that itself holds a possible secret is `a file whose name obid won't show`, as the secret scan names it. A binary file counts 0 and 0; `byFile` lists the first 200 files and the totals count them all. Changes to files git tracks count whether they're committed or not; a new file counts once it's staged or committed, so one the agent left untracked doesn't. The read is in a 60-second, 2 MiB budget: past either, the round records nothing. The job and its events carry the latest round's; `GET /v1/jobs/{id}` adds `rounds`, each round's reading as it was then, oldest first, each with its `round`. Absent before the first round ends.
- Publishing happens by itself. A job that ends `done` with commits not on its pull request (its check passed, or it has none) publishes before it ends, with no device signature: `step` is `"Publishing"` meanwhile, and `publishStep` follows the scan, push and PR creation.
  - It pushes the branch and opens a pull request into `base`, ready for review (never a draft), or pushes to the one already open.
  - The secret scan still gates it. It reads every line every commit adds (including merge commits), every commit's message, and the pull request's title and description, in plain text and in base64 (standard or URL-safe, also wrapped over several lines), hex (also `\x41…` and `0x41, …`) and URL-encoded copies (the rule then reads like `AWS access key, base64`). File findings name the commit whose diff introduced them, and commit-message findings name the commit whose message triggered them. If a file finding is gone from the branch tip but remains in an earlier commit, the fix round asks the agent to make a `--fixup` commit; for a commit-message finding, it asks the agent to make a `--fixup=reword:` commit. Both are autosquashed onto `origin/<base>`, and Publish scans the rewritten history and messages again before anything goes out. The first block always gets that fix round, even on a job with one round (a fixup of the commit that holds it, rewritten onto the base); a later block gets one only while rounds are left and it found something else. A possible secret makes the job `failed` (on obid `interrupted`, the line in `reason`, the agent's summary kept) if the bounded fix round cannot clear it, and its `summary` starts with `Not published: possible secret in <file>:<line> (<rule>).`, where `<file>` is the file, or `commit message`, `pull request title` or `pull request description`. Nothing goes out, and it never says the secret. If the scanner can't run, nothing goes out either.
  - Stopped while publishing (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/stop` or `POST /v1/stop-all`): nothing more goes out. The runner checks right before the push and right before the pull request; a push that already went out is listed in `outgoing`.
  - Only a job that ends while the runner is up, with commits made since its agent started, publishes by itself. A job that ended before the runner started never does, and one whose own publish failed isn't tried again by itself (a round with no new commits keeps its `publishError`).
  - Before publishing, the runner fetches `base` and compares the job's commits with it by patch (`git cherry`, then a trial merge for squash merges). When they're all on it already, nothing goes out: the job stays `done` with `commits` 0, and its `summary` starts with `Not published: its commits are already on <base>.`
  - Any other failure (push, `gh`) leaves the job `done` with `publishError` set. Then Publish by hand is offered (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish`).
  - **On obid** (#627): a push that went out counts, whatever follows. When the pull request then fails (`gh` can't look for it, open it or isn't installed), `pushed` is the commits pushed and `publishError` starts `Pushed, but ` with the pull request's own failure (`Pushed, but couldn't look for the open pull request: <what gh said>`); Publish by hand is still offered, for the pull request. A repository whose `remote` isn't GitHub's (a `file://` one; `pullRequests` in obid only: configuration) gets the push and nothing else: `gh` is never asked, the job ends with `pushed` set, `published` and `publishError` null, and the Publish stage `done` with the detail `Secret scan clean · pushed · no pull request for this remote`.
- `scan` (obid only, #735): how the secret scan of the job's last publish went, `{"ran": true, "clean": true, "commits": 2, "files": 3, "seconds": 4}`, or for a block `{"ran": true, "clean": false, …, "findings": [{"file": "keys.txt", "line": 1, "rule": "AWS access key"}]}`: the file (or `commit message`, `pull request title`, …), the line when it has one, and the rule, never the matched text. `commits` and `files` are what the push would send beyond the base. `{"ran": false, "reason": "checks failed"}` when the job failed at its check, so nothing was scanned; `{"ran": false, "reason": "Not published: the secret scan couldn't run (…)."}` when the scanner couldn't run (then nothing is published, as always), and the same with `publishError` when publishing stopped before the scan. A new publish replaces it. Absent before anything was published or tried.
- `published`: the pull request's URL once published. Use `pipeline` to show publishing, review and merge progress.
- `publishError`: why the runner couldn't publish the job by itself, or null. Show it instead of "Published".
- `pushed`: how many of `commits` the pull request has: all of them when it was last published, or null before then. On obid, how many the remote has once a push went out, with a pull request or not (#627). A published job that ran again (Try again) and ended `done` with new commits pushes them to the same branch by itself, so they land on the open pull request. A round that rebased the branch rewrote its commits: they count from the commit the runner last pushed, so `pushed` drops (to 0 after a full rebase) and the rewritten commits are unpublished until they're pushed, with the same number of commits.
- `summaries`: every round's summary from the agent, oldest first, or null. The pull request's description quotes them all. On obid, a round's summary is its turn's last words: Claude Code's `result`, or, when that is empty (its last message was a tool call), the last text the agent wrote in the turn. A turn whose agent wrote nothing at all adds none, and its job's `summary` stays the earlier round's (#764).
- `outgoing`: everything the runner sent out for the job, oldest first: the job's report lists it. Always a list, empty before anything went out. Each is `{"action": "push", "repo": "deskree-inc/oby", "id": "<commit>", "branch": "oby/fix-75", "date": "2026-10-09T22:01:40Z"}`; `action` is `push` (`id` the commit pushed, `branch` where), `pullRequest` (opened; `id` its URL), `pullRequestEdit` (its description brought up to date; `id` its URL), `checkRun` (Oby's review, or a Mac check's results, written as a check run on the job's pull request; `id` the check run's URL), `comment` (the same from runners before check runs; `id` the comment's URL), `merge` (squash-merged into `dev` by the runner; `id` the pull request's URL, `branch` the branch it deleted), `issueLabels` (issue URL and labels added/removed), `issueClaim`, `issueChecklist`, or `projectStatus` (issue URL and action); older jobs may list `issueAssignment`. `branch` is present on pushes and merges only. More actions may come: show one you don't know as its `action` and `id`. Ids only, never what was sent; the runner's journal keeps one line per action, ids only too.
- What needs you: a job that's `done` with commits the runner couldn't publish (`publishError`), `failed` or `interrupted` (on obid, the one that stopped part way, with `reason` and `nextStep`), or one Oby's review requested changes on for the fourth time (`review`, below), unless it's dismissed. Say so where the apps would otherwise say nothing needs you.
- `dismissed`: when the user let the job go (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/dismiss`), or null. A dismissed job needs nothing: it sends no alert, offers no Publish, and its `pipeline` shows Publish as `skipped` with the detail `Dismissed`. It stays in the list and in history. Telling it something (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`) clears `dismissed`.
- `archived` (obid only): when the job was archived (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/archive`, or the conversation's `oby_archive_job`), or null. An archived job ended and needs nothing; the apps leave it off Home and show it under Archived, as Sangam's design does (`design/app/src/renderer/src/ArchiveView.tsx`). It stays in `GET /v1/jobs` with the key set, and every route still works on it. Unarchiving clears it, and so does running again (Try again, `oby_continue_job`).
- `changes` (obid only): what Oby or the owner changed about the job after it started, oldest first, each `{"what": "Mode changed from Ask to Accept edits by Oby, in a conversation", "date": "…"}`: archived or unarchived, continued, its mode, tool or account changed, by whom (`by Oby, in a conversation`, `by you`, `on your Confirm`). `what` is obid's own words. Always a list, empty when nothing changed. Part of the job's report (`oby_job` reads it).
- `issue`: the GitHub issue the work is about. The runner fetches it for the agent, which has no GitHub login, and the pull request says `Closes #N`, or `Part of #N` when the issue's body has a checklist (a feature or an epic: its items are ticked as slices merge, and it closes once its tests pass) or `gh` can't read it.
- `rounds`: how many rounds the agent may take (1 means no loop); `round`: the one it's on.
- `check`: a one-line command that must succeed for the work to count as done.
  - After each round the runner runs it in the job's worktree. If it fails and rounds are left, the agent gets another round with the command's output and the job's `task`, quoted: if the failure is what the task asked for (a test meant to fail), the agent changes nothing and starts its summary with `FAILS AS ASKED`. A round whose summary starts that way ends the rounds, with no other round to undo it, but it isn't a pass: the job ends needing you, `interrupted` on obid with no `nextStep` (`reason` `` `<check>` fails, as the agent says the task asked for, so nothing was pushed. The branch is kept: tell it what you want. ``), and nothing is published, since only checks that pass let work out, whatever the agent says.
  - A run of the check that takes more than 20 minutes is ended, and fails; its output ends with `Oby stopped it after 20 minutes, the most a test run may take.`, followed by `The last test that started: <name>.` when Swift Testing's or XCTest's output names one.
  - Test integrity: after the check, the runner compares both the job's commits (what Publish would send) and its worktree (what the check ran, uncommitted changes and new files too) with `startCommit` itself, not with a merge base, and fails the round when either clearly weakens the tests: it deletes a test file or moves one out of the tests, adds a skip marker (`.disabled(`, `.enabled(if:`, `XCTSkip`, `it.skip`, `@pytest.mark.skip`, `t.Skip(`, `#[ignore]`…), lowers the number of tests declared or of real assertions in a test file (one commented out or one that can't fail, like `#expect(true)`, doesn't count), adds a line to an existing test file that keeps the tests below it from running (a bare `return`, a block comment left open, `#if false`, `if (false) {`), touches test or CI config (`.github/workflows/`, `jest.config.*`, `pytest.ini`, `conftest.py`…), or changes the test settings in a build file (changes or removes a `.testTarget(…)` in `Package.swift`, however many lines it spans; changes a `test` script or the `jest`, `vitest`, `mocha`, `ava`, `c8` or `nyc` settings in `package.json`, compared as JSON so a value on a line of its own counts; changes pytest's settings in `pyproject.toml` or `setup.cfg`; skips tests or takes out a test target or plan in an Xcode scheme; excludes a test file from its target or takes it out of a target's sources in `project.pbxproj`). The diff ignores what the agent could set up to hide changes: no replacement objects (`refs/replace`) or grafts, no text conversion or external diff, binary files read as text, fixed `a/` and `b/` path prefixes, and `.gitattributes` read from `startCommit`. The agent's next round lists what's wrong, even when the command passed. When git can't say, the round fails too. Other edits to existing tests, and test files moved within the tests, don't fail it: a job that ends `done` says them in its `summary`, under `Changed in the existing tests, for review:`. New tests are fine.
  - Without a check and with more than one round, the agent's own last line decides: `GOAL MET`, or `GOAL NOT MET: …`, which means another round.
- `checks` (obid only, #734): the job's own check, run by run. The job and its events carry the latest run as a one-item list, sent again only when something in it changes (a step starts or ends, a test count or a failing test); `GET /v1/jobs/{id}` has every run, oldest first: `[{"round": 1, "steps": [{"command": "cargo fmt --check", "passed": true, "started": "2026-10-19T09:41:02Z", "seconds": 3, "running": false}, {"command": "cargo test -p obid", "passed": null, "started": "2026-10-19T09:41:05Z", "running": true, "tests": {"passed": 412, "failed": 0, "total": 793}}], "failing": [{"test": "jobs::tests::a", "message": "assertion `left == right` failed"}]}]`. A check's command line is split into steps at its top-level `&&`s, and is one step when it has any other top-level operator (like `;`, `||`, `|` or `&`), a `[[ … ]]` test, a `#` or a heredoc (`<<`); it runs as it would whole, in one shell: obid prints a marker line between steps to time them, which the check's output leaves out. `started` is when a step started; `seconds` is sent once it ended (a running step's would change every second, so the apps count it from `started`). `passed` is null for a step that is running or never ran; a step after the one that failed never runs. `tests` is best-effort, from what `cargo test`, `swift test` and `xcodebuild test` (XCTest and Swift Testing), pytest, jest, vitest and `node --test` print: counted test by test as they run where the runner prints each one, else from its summary; absent for a step whose runner said nothing it reads. `failing` names up to 20 failing tests with their message's first 3 lines (at most 300 characters): a line holding a vault secret or anything the secret scan's rules flag (in any form it reads) is `[possible secret hidden]`. Runs in the same round (the owner's words after a check passed) are kept apart. Absent before the first check runs.
- `limit`: the limit the runner stopped the job at, or null. The runner holds every job to its limits itself; no prompt states them. **On obid no clock ends a job that is making progress** (the owner's direction of 2026-10-19, as Claude Code works): a job runs as long as its work needs, and is held only when it stops making progress:
  - **quiet** (`"no progress for 15 minutes"`): for `stallAfter` (config, in seconds, 900 unless set; 1 to 86,400) the agent wrote nothing at all (no message, no tool call or answer, no streamed text, no command output: a background command the agent started counts while it prints, as Codex's `outputDelta`; Claude Code's background tasks, below) and nothing in the job's worktree changed (a file written or a commit made counts, ignored folders like `target/` too, but never a file dated ahead of now, and a change in the worktree stands for progress for 4 windows in a row at most without a word from the agent: a quiet build is progress, a loop touching a file isn't). A wait for the owner (a permission, a question, a plan to approve) or for the job's Apple check (`oby-check apple`, queued or running on a Mac) never counts as quiet;
  - **loop** (`"the same failing call 5 times"`): the same tool call (the same tool and input, its numbers and spacing aside) failed 5 times with no edit in between (reads and other calls in between don't count; that call passing, or any edit, starts it over). Claude Code's and Codex's jobs; Cursor's adapter doesn't report its calls yet;
  - **turns** (`"200 turns in one round"`): Claude Code's `--max-turns 200` ended the round (`error_max_turns`); Codex and Cursor have no such switch;
  - **ceiling** (`"the job ran 12 hours"`) and **budget** (`"the job used 50000000 tokens"`): safety limits against runaway cost only, never the way a job is meant to end: `jobCeilingHours` (config, 12 unless set, 1 to 168) of the job's running time, every run, restart and Try again added up, and `jobTokenBudget` (config, 50 million unless set) of the tokens its tool counted in `usage`, cache reads left out, checked as each turn reports them. Only the owner's Confirm in the inbox carries a job on past either, and gives it a new ceiling and budget: words for it from anywhere else (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`, which oby-mcp and other sessions call too, a conversation, the event orchestrator) do nothing but ask. The Try-again words bring a `confirm` item titled `Try <title> again`, other words one titled `Start a follow-up of <title>` (a follow-up would start its counters again), and the call answers `422` with `It stopped at its safety limit (<limit>): carrying it on waits for your Confirm in the inbox.`; words the brain or the orchestrator planned are refused as before, or wait for the same Confirm;
  - the same failure twice in a row, 3 actions in a row the runner denied, or rounds run out, as below.
  - **A turn that ends while background work runs isn't the job's end** (obid only). Claude Code's background tasks (Bash's `run_in_background`, Monitor) wake it when they end, as in a terminal: headless, with its input still open, it runs a turn of its own after the task's `task_notification` (claude 2.1.302, 2026-10-20). obid follows its `system` `background_tasks_changed` lines (the whole list of tasks still running each time; its housekeeping, `ambient`, and the tasks a turn waits for itself, `awaited`, left out). When a job's turn ends (`result`) with one still running, obid keeps the agent's input open and waits, `step` `Waiting for <the task's description>`, and the turn's end is that later turn's. A task still running stands for progress as a change in the worktree does: for 4 quiet windows at most without a word from the agent, so a server, a `--watch`, `tail -f` or a persistent Monitor that never ends stalls as **quiet**, as does a turn that doesn't come once the task ended. The ceiling, the budget and Stop hold throughout. A Claude Code that ends before its task does (it exited) is told once, in the same session, to run what it waits for in the foreground; Codex and Cursor, whose background commands wake nothing, are told the same when their last lines say in the first person that they'll come back (`I'll continue when…`, `I'll check back`, `when the monitor reports`). If it ends that way again, the job needs you: `interrupted`, `reason` `Stopped waiting: <its summary's first line> The agent ended its turn to wait for work still running, and nothing wakes it once its turn is over. The branch is kept: Try again picks its session up.` and `nextStep` `Try again`. Such a turn never ends as `Nothing committed`. Its transcript's turn starts with `Oby told <tool> to wait for its work in the foreground` (a `start` entry), its `step` `Oby: wait for it in the foreground`.

  A job held at quiet, loop, turns or ceiling is **stalled**: obid ends the agent's turn and asks it, in one short turn more of its same session (3 minutes at most, and no longer quiet than `stallAfter`), for a handoff note: what's done, what's left, what failed and why. The note is the agent's words, quoted as data, at most 4 KB, without the vault's secrets; a note the secret scan flags anywhere isn't kept at all. When the agent can't write one (or its note was flagged) (it doesn't answer, the session is gone), obid writes it from what it saw (the round, the last step, the commits). The job then needs you (#725): it's `interrupted` with `limit` and `stalled` set (below, the note in `stalled.note`), `reason` `Stalled: <limit>.` and `nextStep` `Try again`, and its `summary` stays the agent's own (one with no session to pick up is `failed`, its `summary` that reason). Its branch and worktree are kept (uncommitted work in the `wip:` commit, as for any job that ends without commits to publish) and nothing is published. The conversation decides what comes next (continue, split or restart): it isn't left waiting silently.
  - **Transient errors are retried, never a failure:** Claude Code retries an overloaded API, a 5xx and a dropped connection itself (10 times, with growing waits) and Codex its dropped streams (5 times). When a turn still ends on one (Claude Code's `API Error: 5xx` or `API Error: 429`, `overloaded`, `Connection error`, `Request timed out`, `stream disconnected`, Codex's `exceeded retry limit`; never the words of a usage limit, and no `429` while the account's usage says it's full), obid runs the round again in the same session after 30 seconds, then 2 minutes, then 5 minutes (never longer than `stallAfter`), telling the agent to pick up where it left off; `step` says `Retrying after <error> (<n> of 3)`. Only a fourth such error in a row, with no turn between them that ended well, fails the job.
- `stalled` (obid only): why and where a stalled job stopped, or null (and null again once it runs on). `{"why": "quiet", "text": "no progress for 15 minutes", "at": "2026-10-19T14:02:10Z", "round": 2, "branch": "oby/abc123-vault-secrets", "commit": "9f2c…", "commits": 3, "session": true, "note": "Done: …\nLeft: …\nFailed: …", "noteBy": "agent"}`. `why` is `quiet`, `loop`, `turns`, `ceiling` or `budget`; `text` is the same as `limit`; `commit` is the job's own last commit (null when it has none; obid's `wip:` commit, when there is one, sits on top of it); `commits` counts the job's own commits as `commits` does; `session` says whether a session is kept to carry on in (its id stays obid's); `note` is the handoff note, `noteBy` `agent` or `obid` (who wrote it). Old jobs and older runners don't send this key.
  - **Continue** in the same session (past a ceiling or budget, only through the owner's Confirm, above): `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages` with the Try-again words (the conversation's try-again action), as One more round: the same worktree, branch and session, one round more, `limit` and `stalled` cleared, and the agent told to pick up where it left off.
  - **Restart fresh** from the note: `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages` with the owner's other words, a follow-up (`followUpOf`) whose worktree starts from the job's branch when it has commits and whose first prompt quotes its report, the note included; or **split**: new jobs (`POST /v1/jobs`, `followUpOf` and `startFrom` naming it) for each piece.
  - A `stalled` event comes once when a job stalls, with the job (`data: { …Job }`), beside its `job` event.

  The Swift runner (Engine/Guards.swift) still holds these limits instead:
  - a round's turn may take 45 minutes (a turn the user's words start between rounds, while the check runs, too), a whole job 3 hours (every round and check included). The round's clock stands still while an Apple check the agent asked for (`oby-check apple`) is queued or running (one the job's final check asked for first too, once the agent asks for it), however long it waits: the job's 3 hours still count it. When the round runs out, the runner ends the agent; if the work is all committed, the agent's latest Apple check of the last commit didn't fail, and the check passes on that commit, the job ends `done` and publishes as any finished job does (its `summary` starts with `The round ran past 45 minutes before …`, `limit` stays null, and the review still gates the merge). Otherwise, or without a check, it's held as below;
  - Claude Code runs with `--max-turns 200`, which counts each message's turns, so each round's (checked with Claude Code 2.1.296); Codex has no such switch, so the timer alone holds it;
  - rounds run out (the Lead's jobs get 4: one attempt and three fix rounds);
  - no progress: the same failure twice in a row (the failing tests' ids, read from all of the check's output, plus its last 20 lines, with times, durations, addresses and ids taken out), or 3 actions in a row the runner denied.

  Such a job is `failed` with `limit` set (on obid `interrupted`, `nextStep` `One more round`, `reason` `Stopped at a limit: <limit>. The branch is kept and nothing was pushed.`, and `summary` the agent's own) (like `"the same failure twice in a row"` or ``"`swift test` still fails after 4 rounds"``), keeps its branch and pushes nothing. On the Swift runner its `summary` starts with `Stopped at a limit: <limit>. The branch is kept and nothing was pushed; one more round is possible.` and the Mac window says `Stopped at a limit: <title>`. One more round: its inbox item (kind `failed`) offers `One more round` instead of `Try again`, or `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`; either clears `limit`. Stopping a job (or a limit stopping it) also ends a check that's still running. Older runners don't send this key.
- `startCommit`: the commit the work started from (the base on origin, or the pull request's head), recorded when the worktree is set up, or null before then and on older jobs. Test integrity compares with it: an agent can move a branch or a ref, not this. Older runners don't send this key.
- `firstSwiftBuildSeconds`: how long the job's first `swift build` check took, in whole seconds, or null when the job has not run one. The runner includes `First swift build: …` in the finished job's summary. Older runners don't send this key.
- `model`: a model the user chose for this job (the stronger one on the plan's card, or one it was moved to), or null for the one in Settings › Models.
- `complexity`: the brain's `routine` or `complex` classification, or null on older and internal jobs. `effort` is the reasoning effort this job's turns use; complex dispatches use that tool's `complexEffort`, routine dispatches and review/verdict jobs use `effort`. A move selects the destination tool's effort for the saved complexity. Complex job reports name the effort; this field records it for every job.
- `moves`: every time the user moved the job to another tool, account or model (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/move`), oldest first. Always a list, empty when it never moved. Each is `{"from": "Codex · Deskree", "to": "Claude Code · Personal", "why": "Codex keeps looping", "date": "2026-10-11T14:02:10Z"}`: `from` and `to` are the tool and the account, with the model after another ` · ` when the job ran one of its own (`"Claude Code · Personal · Fable 5.1"`); `why` is what the user said when they moved it, or null. The job screens show the latest as "Moved from Codex · Deskree to Claude Code · Personal". On obid, `oby_change_job` and its Confirm add one (`why` null) when they change a finished job's tool or account; obid has no `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/move` yet.
- `connectionUses`: every call the job's agent made to a connected app through the broker (Connected apps, below), oldest first. Always a list, empty before the first. Each records the job, connection, tool, `read` or `write`, whether it was allowed, the vault secret's name, host and date; it never records a value or request or response body.
- `notes`: what the user told the agent along the way (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`), oldest first, or null.
- `routine` (obid only): on a job a routine's run started, `{"id", "name", "scheduled", "catchUp"}` ([obid only: routines](#obid-only-routines-395)), else null.
- `supervised` (obid only): what the conversation that owns the job did by itself each time it stalled, oldest first (obid only: the conversation supervises its jobs).
- `activity` (obid only, #730): what the agent did, at a glance, from its transcript (`GET /v1/jobs/{id}/transcript`); absent until the job's first tool call. It's saved and sent with the job's `job` event at most once a second while the agent works, and once more when its turn ends.
  - `counts`: how many calls of each kind, over the whole job: `read` (Read, Glob, LS), `search` (Grep), `edit` (Edit, Write, MultiEdit, NotebookEdit), `command` (Bash), `test` (a command that is the job's `check`, alone or as the last step of a `&&` chain; a command that only looks like a test runner is a `command`), `tetrix` (a Tetrix MCP tool), `web` (WebSearch, WebFetch) and `mcp` (any other MCP tool). A call's kind comes from what the tool reports, never from words the agent writes: Claude Code's tool name, Codex's item type, Cursor's call `kind`. So a Cursor call obid can't place (an MCP tool among them) counts nowhere, and neither do helpers.
  - `groups`: the session in a few parts, the latest 20. A group starts at each of the agent's own words between tool calls, and is titled by their first sentence (at most 80 characters, "Found where a blocked scan ends the job"), with `by: "agent"`: these are the agent's words, quoted, so show them as such. Calls with no words before them group by kind under obid's own title, with `by: "obid"`: "Read 14 files", "Edited 2 files", "Searched the code 3 times", "Ran 4 commands", "Ran the tests", "Asked Tetrix", "Looked on the web" or "Used 2 tools". A group takes at most 50 calls; the next starts a group of its own. A round's start and the owner's words end a group. No model writes a title.
  - Each group: `id`, `title`, `by`, `state` (`running` while a call runs, `failed` when its last call failed, else `done`), `started`, `finished` (once it isn't running), `counts` (as above, its own), `files` (what its calls read or changed, at most 20: from the worktree, or only the file's name when it's outside it), `diff` (`{added, removed}`, the lines its edits added and removed, when it has any) and `tools`, each `{tool, kind, summary, state, started, seconds}`: a running group's last 10, a finished one's last 3.
  - The secrets obid holds are out of titles and file names as they're out of the transcript. The whole is at most 32 KB: past that, the oldest groups are left out.
  ```json
  {"counts": {"read": 5, "search": 0, "edit": 2, "command": 0, "test": 1, "tetrix": 0, "web": 0, "mcp": 0},
   "groups": [{"id": "g2", "title": "Looking for where a blocked scan ends the job", "by": "agent", "state": "done", "started": "2026-10-19T08:13:52Z", "finished": "2026-10-19T08:14:30Z", "counts": {"read": 5, …}, "files": ["src/scan.rs", "src/jobs.rs"], "tools": [{"tool": "Read", "kind": "read", "summary": "Reading lib.rs", "state": "done", "started": "2026-10-19T08:14:29Z", "seconds": 0.4}]}]}
  ```
- `context` (obid only, #730): what the agent is doing now and why, in its latest words between tool calls (at most 200 characters, its secrets out); absent until it has said something. They're the agent's words, quoted, never obid's: `contextBy` is always `"agent"` beside it. Show `step` when it's absent. Updated with `activity`.
- `usage` (obid only, #731): what the job's turns used, summed over its turns and rounds and kept after it ends: `{"input": 220, "output": 50, "cacheRead": 2000, "cacheWrite": 100, "costUsd": 0.5, "priced": "plan"}`. `input` is the input not read from the cache. Each key is there only when the tool reported it (never 0 for a count it didn't report), and the key itself is absent until a turn reports any. Claude Code: each `result` line's `modelUsage` summed over its models (its `usage` when there's none) and `total_cost_usd`; those are the session's so far, earlier turns resumed with `--resume` included, so each turn counts what grew since the session's last result. Codex: `thread/tokenUsage/updated` (each round's own use of its thread, while it runs); no cache writes or cost. Cursor: a `session/prompt` answer's `usage`, when it has one. `priced`: `plan` when a subscription covers it (Claude Code's `apiKeySource` `none`; Codex's `account/read` type `chatgpt`, asked once per account by a job's run, with `refreshToken: false`, and kept with the account's usage until it signs in again; only its type is read), `api` when an API key pays per token, absent when the tool doesn't say.
- `windowShare` (obid only, #732): the job's estimated share of its account's windows, in percentage points of each: `{"fiveHour": 11.0, "sevenDay": 3.5, "estimate": true}` ("46% used · this job ≈ 11%"). Each time the account reports its usage (`fiveHour`, `sevenDay` on the Account), each window's rise since its last report is divided among the jobs that were running on the account at that report too, by their tokens since (`usage`, every count summed); evenly when none reported tokens. A job alone gets the whole rise, so the owner's own use of the account while it runs counts as its. A job's first report only marks where it starts. A window that resets starts the job's sum over, so it's always the share of the current window, or of the last one the job saw. A window no report rose in is null; the key is absent until the job has a share. Always an estimate (`estimate: true`).
- `followUpOf` (obid only): the job this one follows up, or null. Words for a job that ended start a follow-up (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`): the apps show its card under the first job's thread, after the words, as Sangam's design does ("Started a follow-up").
- `startWhen`: what the job waits for before it starts (Start when, below), as a list of conditions, or null for a job that starts at once and once it started. `waiting`: why a queued job isn't running yet (#238's shape), or null: while it waits for its `startWhen`, `{"kind": "limit", "text": "for Codex · Deskree: 5-hour window resets at 4:59 PM", "job": null}`. `kind` is `limit` when the first thing it still waits for is an account's room, else `schedule`; `text` follows "Waiting", as `step` says it whole (`"Waiting for Codex · Deskree: 5-hour window resets at 4:59 PM"`); `job` is the first job it still waits for, by id, or null. `reserved`, only while it waits for room (`{"room": true}`, Start when) that just accounts in their weekly reserve could give: those accounts' ids, on each of which the apps offer `Use Personal anyway (82% of week)` (`POST /v1/accounts/{id}/reserve`); left out otherwise. It's null once the job isn't queued. Grouping waiting jobs under Waiting in the apps comes with #238. Older runners don't send these keys.
- `title`: what to call the job in rows, the job screen, the Mac window and alerts. It's the brain's short title (3 to 6 words, like `"Mac runner notifications"`), which every planned dispatch gets and the job keeps. Jobs without one (the rules' plans when the brain is down, an epic's sub-issue jobs, jobs from before titles) get `"<Kind> <repo name> #<number>"` when `pr` or `issue` is set, otherwise the first line of `task` (falling back to `request`), capped at 60 characters. It's always a string; older runners don't send it, so clients fall back the same way. Show "Round 2 of 5" when `rounds` is above 1.
- `report`: whether `GET /v1/jobs/{id}/report` has a saved report. Research jobs also carry `reads` (up to five named repositories) and `readCommits` (their pinned snapshots). Report Markdown and proposals are returned only by the report endpoint, not in job events.
- `artifacts` (obid only): what the job made to be looked at, `[{"id": "<artifact id>", "version": 2}]`, one per entry of its `.obi/artifacts/`, published when the job ends, and `artifactProblems`: why an entry wasn't, `["leak.md: Not published: the secret scan found …"]`. Both `[]` when there's none ([obid only: artifacts](#obid-only-artifacts)).
- `files`: the files or folders (ending in `/`) the job is expected to touch, relative to the repository: its claim (below). From the Lead's brief, from the client that started it (`POST /v1/jobs`), or else from its issue: the issue's `Files:` line (paths and folders, in backticks or separated by commas), or when it has none, the paths its title and text name (in backticks, or with a folder, like `Engine/Checks.swift` or `MCP/`). The runner reads the issue as it plans, so a job `POST /v1/plans` starts has them from the start; a job that still has none claims them when its worktree gets ready. Null when nothing named any (the job then neither waits nor blocks), `[]` when the client claimed none. `lead` is set on jobs the Lead started (below), null otherwise: the goal (one Lead turn) that started it. `claim` also reports issue-sync errors on any job; successful changes are listed in `outgoing`. Older runners don't send these keys.
- `question`: what the job's agent asked the user, `{"text": "Should a job that commits nothing end as failed, or get a new status?", "options": ["Failed", "A new status"]}` (`options` may be empty), or null, with `details` (what each option does, or null) and `recommended` (the index of the option the agent recommends, or null) when the agent gave them. Agents are told to end their summary with `QUESTION: …` and optionally `OPTIONS: <label> — <what choosing it does> | …` rather than bury a decision in their report, the way Claude Code asks: 2 or 3 short labels, each followed by ` — ` and one plain sentence, the recommended one first with `(Recommended)` after its label (taken off the label, which is what the answer sends back). Descriptions are kept only when every option has one; the runner reads those last lines when a turn ends. Markdown around the label (`**QUESTION:**`) is ignored; the question and its options keep every character, like `*.swift`. The job then waits for the answer: it stays `running` with `step` `"Waiting for your answer"`, its agent idle, and its round doesn't move on (no check runs). Other jobs keep going. The question lands in the inbox (below) and alerts the phone. The answer reaches the agent as its next message, exactly as `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages` would send it, and `question` goes back to null. Telling the job anything else answers it too; stopping it drops it. A job whose agent exits while its question waits is `interrupted` and keeps `question`: nothing is checked or published until it's answered, and answering picks its session up again. Across a runner restart it keeps waiting instead: still `running` with `step` `"Waiting for your answer"` and its `question`, nothing running until the answer picks its session up again, in the same round. Neither is recorded as a failure. Older runners don't send the key.
  - `plan`: an agent's plan waiting for approval, in Markdown, or null (Permission modes, below). Older runners don't send it.
- `tetrix`: what the runner saw of the job's agent and Tetrix (#130), or null before its tool said (and on older jobs):

  ```json
  {"connected": true, "calls": {"search_entities": 2, "get_entity": 1, "save_session": 1}, "rounds": [4],
   "reminded": null, "stamps": {"commits": 2, "stamped": 2, "sessions": ["ses_8f2a"]}, "author": null}
  ```

  `connected`: whether the agent had Tetrix's MCP server connected, from Claude Code's stream-json `init` (`mcp_servers`, a server named for Tetrix with `status` `connected`) or Codex's app-server (`mcpServerStatus/list`: one with tools that isn't `notLoggedIn`); a call to a Tetrix tool counts as connected too. Null when the tool didn't say. `calls`: the agent's calls to Tetrix's tools by name, over every round (Claude Code's `tool_use` named `mcp__<server>__<tool>`, Codex's `mcpToolCall` items, where the server's name holds `tetrix`). `rounds`: Tetrix calls in each round that ended, oldest first, or null. `reminded`: true once a round that had Tetrix connected and called none of it got a reminder in the next round's message (once a job). `stamps`: the job's commits since `startCommit` and how many carry a `Tetrix-Session: <id>` trailer, with the sessions they name, read when its last round ends done, or null. `author`: on Oby's review jobs, what the runner saw of the author's agent, as the reviewer is told; else null.
  - Agents are told to start by asking Tetrix about the job's issue or pull request and the area it touches (`search_entities`, `get_entity`, `code_impact` where the repository's code is indexed) before reading many files, and to end with `save_session` and the `Tetrix-Session:` trailer on their commits. Among MCP tools, agents may call only Tetrix's graph reads and `save_session`.
  - When the job ends, its `summary` ends with a line like `Tetrix: 4 calls (search_entities 2, get_entity 1, save_session 1). Session saved. 2 of 2 commits stamped Tetrix-Session (ses_8f2a).`, or `Tetrix: connected, but the agent didn't use it.`, or `Tetrix: not connected for this account's agent, so it wasn't asked. …`. Not on review jobs: their last line is the verdict. Older runners don't send the key.
- `mode`: how much the job asks the user, `ask`, `acceptEdits`, `plan` or `bypass` (Permission modes, below); obid also has `sandbox`, its default (obid only: permissions answered without the owner, below). Jobs from before modes are `bypass`. On obid, `review` and `investigate` jobs are always `plan` (Permission modes, below). Older runners don't send the key.
- `permission`: the command or edit the job's agent waits for the user's OK on, or null:

  ```json
  {"id": "p1a2b3c", "tool": "Bash", "title": "Run swift test", "detail": "swift test --filter SpeechTests",
   "rule": "Bash(swift test:*)", "always": "Yes, and don't ask again for swift test in deskree-inc/oby"}
  ```

  On obid it may also carry `why` (obid only: permissions answered without the owner, below). `tool` is Claude Code's tool name (Codex's commands are `Bash`, its file changes `Edit`; on obid a Codex command comes out of the shell Codex wraps it in, `/bin/zsh -lc '…'` or `/bin/zsh -lc "…"`, before it's judged or shown, #634). `title` is what the card asks ("Run swift test", "Edit Jobs.swift", "Fetch example.com"), `detail` the whole command, the files (relative to the repository) or the address. `rule` is what "always" saves, in Claude Code's syntax, and `always` the sentence to show for it; both are null when the runner can't make a safe rule (a command chained with `&&`, `;` or a pipe, or one that runs whatever follows it: a shell, an interpreter or a wrapper like `bash -c`, `python3`, `node`, `env`, `sudo`, `xargs`, `uv run`, `npm exec`): then it can only be allowed once, and on obid its `title` is `Run` and the whole command (up to 2,000 characters, as `detail`), never cut, since the inbox card shows only the title. While it's set the job stays `running` with `step` `"Waiting for your OK"`, its agent idle, and its round and job clocks paused: waiting doesn't count toward the 45-minute round or the 3-hour job. Other jobs keep going. It lands in the inbox (kind `permission`) and alerts the phone and the Watch. Answer it with `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/permission`, the inbox, or in words. When it's answered, the agent's next waiting prompt (if any) takes its place. Stopping the job drops it. A job whose agent ends while one waits (the runner restarted) is `interrupted` and keeps `permission`: answering it picks the session up again and tells the agent the answer. Older runners don't send the key.
- **Jobs run side by side, at most `maxRunning` at once** (#237, `GET /v1/status`, `PUT /v1/settings/jobs`, 6 unless changed). A job whose worktree is ready starts when fewer than `maxRunning` jobs are running and no older job waits; otherwise it waits, `queued`, with `step` `Waiting for a free slot: 6 of 6 jobs running`, and the waiting jobs start oldest first as slots free up. Every job whose agent is running takes a slot: Oby's reviews, and the rounds of a job that started already (a review's changes, a rebase, a failed Mac check, the user's words), which never wait for one, so the count can briefly pass the cap. A job waiting for the user's answer takes none. Before a job that waited starts, its branch moves to its base as GitHub has it now (`step` `Bringing <branch> up to date with dev`), so it works on top of what merged meanwhile: only while its worktree has no work of its own, and never on a pull request's branch. When `dev` can't be fetched, it's `failed` with `Couldn't bring <branch> up to date with dev: <why>` rather than start on stale code. Account routing still picks each job's account.
- **`files` is the job's claim, and holds nothing back.** Jobs whose `files` overlap in the same repository (the same path, or one inside a folder the other names) run side by side, each in its own worktree; conflicts are settled when they merge. A claim lasts while the job is queued or running, and after, while its pull request is open or its commits wait to be published (#209); it ends when the pull request is merged or closed, the user dismisses or stops the job, or the job ends with nothing to publish. It does two things. The agent's prompt names the in-flight jobs whose claims overlap its own, with their titles, ids, branches and pull requests, and asks it to keep its change small and expect a rebase. And when two approved pull requests overlap, the older job's merges first: the newer one's Mac check stays queued while the older one's is queued, running or passed and not merged yet, then it's checked, and a conflict with what merged is rebased (Mac check, below).
- `waiting`: why a `queued` job hasn't started (#238), `{"kind": "cap", "text": "a slot opens"}`, or null when nothing holds it (its worktree is getting ready, it's being brought up to date, or it's about to start) and on every job that isn't `queued`. `text` is what every app shows after **Waiting**, like "Waiting · a slot opens"; `job` is the job it waits behind, for `job` and `files`, else absent. `kind` is one of:
  - `cap`: every slot is taken (`maxRunning`, above): `"a slot opens"`. Its `step` reads `Waiting for a free slot: 6 of 6 jobs running`.
  - `update`: the runner is draining for an update (below): `"runner update"`. Its `step` reads `Waiting for runner update`.
  - `schedule`, `limit`: what its `startWhen` waits for (Start when, below): another job, a pull request or a time (`schedule`), or an account's room (`limit`). Its `step` says the same, like `Waiting for Cards screen (#250) to merge`.
  - `job`, `files`: another job, or its claim. Jobs on the same files run side by side (#237), so this runner doesn't set them.

  The apps list a job with `waiting` set under **Waiting**, with a gray `Waiting` badge, between **Working** (running jobs, and queued ones getting ready, badged `Starting`) and **Needs you**. Counts and headlines count running jobs as working, and waiting ones apart: "1 agent is working, 14 waiting." Older runners don't send the key: their queued jobs read as getting ready.

### Start when

A job can wait for something before it starts (#216): another job or a pull request to finish, an account to have room, or a time. Its `startWhen` lists what must all hold, each one of:

- `{"job": "ab12cd34", "is": "done"}`: after another job, by id. `is` is `done` (it ended done), `merged` (its pull request was merged), `published` (its pull request is out) or `ended` (it ended in any way); left out, `done`.
- `{"pr": 205, "is": "merged"}`: after a pull request in the job's repository. `is` is `merged` or `closed` (closed in any way, a merge too); left out, `merged`.
- `{"account": "<id or name>", "window": "fiveHour", "under": 0.5}`: once the account has room in its 5-hour (`fiveHour`) or weekly (`sevenDay`) window: its use is under `under` (a share above 0 and up to 1; left out, 0.8, the share routing calls busy). A window whose reset time has passed counts as empty (Claude Code reports usage only during a job, so its last share would stay), so `"do this on Codex once its 5-hour window resets"` starts at the reset, or at once when Codex has room already. Not while the account is signed out or stopped at its limit. The runner keeps every account's last usage, reset times and limit in `usage.json`, so a restart doesn't forget a window it waits on (Claude Code reports usage only during a job).
- `{"account": "<id or name>", "onPace": true}`: once the account's weekly use is back on pace (Account, `isAhead` false: its share of the week used is no more than the share of the week gone).
- `{"room": true, "agent": "claude"}`: once routing has an account for it, of that tool (`claude`, `codex` or `cursor`; left out, any). Its `account` until then is one it may run on; when this holds it runs on the account routing picks then, as if just sent, and its report says why. A request that only accounts in their weekly reserve could take starts with this (below, Routing): `waiting.kind` is `limit` and `step` says `"Waiting for room: Personal and Codex are in their weekly reserve"`, or `"Waiting for an account with room"` when no lift would help.
- `{"at": "2026-10-13T09:00:00-03:00"}`: at a time, ISO 8601 with its offset.

At most 5. The runner checks them when the job is dispatched (`422` with why: an unknown job, account or tool, the job itself, a share out of range, two accounts, or room with an account), and keeps accounts by id. A job waiting on an account runs on it: `account` and `agent` are that account's. Picking another account on the plan card, or moving the job, drops waiting for room: it runs where it was put.

The job gets its worktree ready, then waits `queued`, with `waiting` and `step` saying what for, joining every condition still to hold with ", and": `"Waiting for Cards screen (#250) to merge, and until 9:00 AM"`. It doesn't count as running on its account, takes no slot (`maxRunning`), and its `files` claim holds from when it was queued, like any queued job's. The runner checks every minute, and as soon as any job ends: pull requests it waits on (and the pull request of a job it waits to see merged) are read from GitHub then. Once everything holds, `startWhen` goes back to null, it's brought up to date with its base and starts (or waits for a slot), and its report says `Started when what it waited for came: …`. It keeps waiting across runner restarts, however many: it never ran, so it isn't picked up like a job whose agent crashed. What changes while its worktree gets ready (started now, told to wait for something else) holds once it's ready. The Mac's, the iPhone's and the Watch's job screens have Start now, and the Watch's list says `Scheduled, waiting …` where it would say Starting.

A condition that can never hold (the job it waits on failed, was stopped or ended with nothing published; the pull request was closed without merging; the account is gone) makes it a decision: `step` is `"Waiting for you: Broken build failed, so it won't be done."` and `question` asks `"It can't start as planned: …. Start it anyway, cancel it, or wait for something else?"` with the options `Start anyway`, `Cancel` and `Wait for something else`, as a `question` inbox item. `Start anyway` starts it now; `Cancel` stops it; `Wait for something else` keeps it waiting, unasked (a failed job tried again, a pull request reopened, may still come), until you change what it waits for (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/start-when`), start it or stop it. Other words are refused (`422`). After a restart it asks again.

A waiting job can be started now (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/start`), told to wait for something else (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/start-when`), moved (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/move`: what it waited for on an account it left goes, and with nothing left it starts; a new model on the same account keeps it) or stopped (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/stop`). In words, the brain fills `startWhen` on the job it plans (`POST /v1/plans`), which then starts waiting: "do this on Codex when it resets", "after the cards job merges", "once #205 is merged", "tomorrow at 9", "when Personal has room".

### Job pipeline and pull request following

Every job response (list, detail, dispatch, stop, message, wait, plan and `job` SSE event) includes `pipeline`, derived in Engine from the job's execution facts and last observed PR state. Clients render it instead of reconstructing stages from `step` text.

- `pipeline.stages`: ordered `prepare`, `work`, optional `check`, `publish`, `review`, `merged`. Each has `id`, `title`, `state`, optional `detail` and optional `reason`. States are `done`, `current`, `upcoming`, `failed`, `skipped`; every failed stage includes a reason. Check is absent when no check command is configured. Work includes the round and live agent step. Publish includes secret scan, push and PR creation progress.
- **Stage times (obid only, #733).** A stage the job reached also has `started` (RFC 3339) and `waited` (seconds paused on the owner: a permission, a question or a plan to approve, as of the job's last change), and, once it's no longer current, `finished` (RFC 3339) and `seconds` (the time it ran, every round's added up, `waited` left out). A current stage has no `seconds`, since it would change every second: the apps count it from `started`. A stage a later round enters again keeps its first `started`, and `finished` goes until it ends again. Time a queued job waits for a free slot counts in no stage's `seconds`. An upcoming or current stage may have `estimate`, `{"low": 120, "high": 780}` in seconds, from the job's `estimate` (below); the Check stage's is the last full run (every step passed) of the same check command in the repository. Stages a job never reached have none of these.
- `estimate` (obid only, #733): what the whole job should take from Prepare to Publish (waiting on people left out), `{"low": 720, "high": 1200, "basis": "From the last 14 jobs like this in obi on Claude Code (13m in the middle).", "samples": 14, "firstGiven": {"low": 660, "high": 1200}}`, in seconds. It's the middle 80% (10th to 90th percentile) of the last 50 jobs done in the same repository with the same agent and kind; with fewer than 5 of those, the range of the repository's last 50 jobs done, and `basis` says so: `"Too early to say: 2 jobs like this so far. Jobs in obi take 4m–19m."`. `samples` is how many jobs like this it read. The time Plan mode spent reading before its plan isn't counted. obid computes it again whenever a stage starts or ends while the job runs; `firstGiven` is the first range it gave, kept so a finished job can show it next to what it took. The history is the finished jobs obid keeps (their stage times, in `home/jobs/`): obid has no SQLite of its own. Absent when the repository has no finished job with stage times yet.
- `pipeline.number` and `pipeline.total`: one-based position and count of displayed stages, including skipped ones. Display “Stage n of m”. The position is the first current/failed stage, otherwise the last completed/skipped stage. A completed investigation with no commits skips Publish, Review and Merged and reports 5 of 5. A failure/cancellation marks its execution stage failed with its reason and skips downstream stages.
- A done, published job progresses through Review: waiting (`current`), changes requested (`failed`, with reason), approved (`done`, with merge current). A merged PR completes Review (`Approved`, or `Merged without review` when GitHub has no review decision) and the final stage; a closed, unmerged PR fails Review and skips Merged. The merge title uses GitHub's actual base, normally “Merged into dev”. Running again shows Work before the earlier round's PR progress.
- `phase` (optional): persisted execution phase `prepare`, `work`, `check` or `publish`. `publishStep` (optional): `scan`, `push` or `pullRequest`. These are execution facts, not separate client derivation rules. Older saved jobs have neither; Engine derives their best available progress.
- `pullRequest`: null until a successful read, otherwise `{"state":"OPEN","reviewDecision":"REVIEW_REQUIRED","base":"dev","mergedAt":null,"checkedAt":"2026-10-09T21:43:30Z"}`. State is `OPEN`, `CLOSED` or `MERGED`; review decision is `REVIEW_REQUIRED`, `CHANGES_REQUESTED`, `APPROVED`, or absent/null (no decision yet). `mergedAt` is present only after a merge. `head` is the pull request's head commit as GitHub has it (absent on older observations): one Oby didn't push is an intervention (Failure record, below). All dates are ISO 8601.
- The runner polls published PRs on startup and every three minutes while open/unknown, using its own `gh pr view` outside the agents' sandbox. It follows `published`, not the input `pr`, including saved jobs older than the latest 50. Closed and merged PRs stop polling. Fresh saved observations wait for the next interval after restart.
- `pullRequestError`: null after a successful refresh; otherwise a short retry message. Network/authentication/JSON failures preserve the last successful observation and retry at the next interval. `checkedAt` remains the date of that last success. Updates are saved and sent as `job` events without overwriting a concurrent agent step or resumed round.
- Following is read-only: it never posts a review, pushes, or merges (merging is the Mac check's, below). [`gh pr view` JSON fields](https://cli.github.com/manual/gh_pr_view) supply only typed status data; GitHub prose never enters an agent prompt.
- Older clients ignore these additive fields. New clients accept an absent pipeline from an older runner and show its ordinary status/step instead.

### Oby's review

When a job publishes (or pushes new commits to its pull request), the runner has the pull request reviewed by one of its own jobs, on a different account than the author's: it prefers the other tool (Claude Code reviews Codex's work, Codex reviews Claude Code's; a Cursor job's pull request goes to Claude Code, else Codex, and Cursor never reviews), takes only an account that's signed in with room on its limits (under 95% of its 5-hour window and under 80% of its weekly one, which keeps the rest for jobs the user sends to it by name), preferring one running ahead of its weekly pace, since a review is short (Routing, under Account), and runs it on that account's model from Settings › Models. With no such account, the runner falls back, last, to the author's own account when it's signed in and under 95% of both windows (#408): the review is still a new job in a new session of its own, with its own worktree and the pull request quoted as data, and never resumes or reads the author's session. It says so: the review's `account` carries `sameAccount: true`, its check run is titled `Oby review · Codex · Deskree (same account as the author): Approved`, and the job's review line in the Lead's and the brain's briefings names it the same way. Reviews still prefer another account and the other tool: the fallback is taken only when none of them has room. A security review never uses it; it needs a different account and waits for one. With no account at all, nothing reviews and `reviewError` says so.

When the PR diff changes a security surface (`Runner/API.swift`, `Runner/Gateway.swift`, `Runner/Devices.swift`, `Runner/Routes.swift`, `Runner/Auth*`, `apps/shared/E2E*` (and `Shared/E2E*`, from branches cut before it moved), `Engine/Vault*`, `Engine/Jobs.swift`, `Engine/Publish*`, anything under `cloud/`, or a path naming auth, tokens, keys, secrets, crypto, listeners or routes), Oby starts a separate security review after the general review approves. It writes an independent `Oby security review` check run. Its reviewer examines privilege escalation, route reachability, token/key and crypto handling, injection, agent/log exposure and unsandboxed Mac access. Every authentication or route-reachability claim must include a HummingbirdTesting or test-server curl probe with command and output. Only likely exploitable HIGH or MEDIUM findings block; denial of service, rate limits and theoretical issues do not. If Git cannot read the changed paths from an existing author worktree, Oby conservatively requires the security pass. Both verdicts must approve before a Mac check can gate the merge.

In chat, asking to review an Oby-authored pull request again routes through its author job (`ask_review_again`), so the verdict and any Mac check stay with that job. Swift rejects a standalone `review` dispatch for a pull request that already has an Oby author job. A manually requested follow-up grants one additional review when the normal review cap has been reached. A review job that makes commits ends failed with `A review made commits; nothing was published`; `Publish` also refuses every `kind: review` job.

Oby runs up to three regular reviews (`Review.maxRounds`). If the third still requests changes, it gives the fourth review to the strongest configured model with account capacity. That escalation reads the whole pull request and all three earlier reviews, then approves or sends its final blockers to the user. It does not start another author round. A changes-requested result at the escalation creates one inbox card per PR head, with Open the PR, One more round and Dismiss. The card says `Oby's reviews are done: review #N yourself`; another published head gets a new card. Reviews triggered by a Mac check, conflict, Xcode Cloud or the user count toward these four. When the runner starts, commits refused under an earlier cap (`reviewError` "Oby reviewed it N times already…") still get reviewed.

- **The review job** is an ordinary `Job` in the list and the event stream: `kind` `review`, `pr` the published pull request's number, `branch` like `oby/review-80`, `task` the author's task, and `reviewOf` the author job's `id` (null on every other job). It reads the diff and the author's task, makes no commits and never publishes. Its `summary` is the review; its last line is the verdict (`VERDICT: APPROVED` or `VERDICT: CHANGES REQUESTED`).
- A first review checks the issue's acceptance criteria and completion tests, bugs in the change and called code, repository rules, and end-to-end behavior on each affected platform in one pass.
