`publicKey` is the P-256 public key, X9.63 (65 bytes, uncompressed), base64. It returns `{"id": "d7c1…"}`. Only the runner's owner can register (the tailnet header); the runner lists devices in its log.

- A key the runner knows returns its id right away, so the app registers every time it connects.
- A new key needs pairing open: the owner runs `touch $OBY_HOME/pairing` on the runner's machine, and the device registers within 10 minutes. That key uses it up, so the iPhone and the Mac each need it opened once. Otherwise `403` with that one line to show: the Mac shows it in Settings › Runner, with Try again.
- Why: agents run on the runner's machine, so through its tailnet address they arrive with the owner's header. They can't touch `pairing`, because `OBY_HOME` is empty inside their bubblewrap.

### `POST /v1/activities`

Hands the runner a Live Activity push token, so it can keep the phone's Lock Screen and Dynamic Island current while the app is suspended:

```json
{"token": "80a1f2…", "kind": "update", "topic": "com.deskree.oby", "environment": "development"}
```

- `token`: the push token in hex. `kind`: `"update"` for one activity's token, `"start"` for the app's push-to-start token (a newer one replaces the older for the same app and environment). `topic`: the app's bundle ID. `environment`: `"development"` (a build from Xcode) or `"production"` (TestFlight and the App Store).
- It returns `{"push": true}` when the runner pushes, `{"push": false}` when it has no APNs key (the token isn't kept). `422` for a token, kind, topic or environment that isn't one of those.

Two seconds after a job changes, the runner pushes what its jobs look like then (one Live Activity, as the app shows it):

- **A job is active** and the runner has update tokens: an `update` with the newest active job (priority 5, skipped when nothing the card shows changed).
- **A job is active**, no update tokens, and it hasn't started one yet: a push-to-start (`start`, priority 10, with an alert) to the start tokens. The phone then hands over the new activity's update token.
- **Nothing is active:** an `end` with how the last job finished and a dismissal 15 minutes after it finished, with no alert (a job that needs you says so with its own notification, below); then the update tokens are dropped.

Apple's answer `410` or `BadDeviceToken` drops a token. The runner keeps tokens in `apns/tokens.json`.

### `POST /v1/notifications`

Hands the runner the app's device token, so it can tell the phone when a job needs you:

```json
{"token": "5b0e9c…", "topic": "com.deskree.oby", "environment": "production"}
```

- `token`: the device token from `registerForRemoteNotifications`, in hex. `topic` and `environment` as for `/v1/activities`. The app sends it on every launch once notifications are allowed (it asks the first time something said on the phone starts a job).
- It returns `{"push": true}` or `{"push": false}`, and `422`, as `/v1/activities` does.

Two seconds after a job changes, the runner sends an alert push (`apns-push-type: alert`, the app's bundle ID as topic, priority 10) for each job whose need changed. Nothing else notifies: steps, rounds, and jobs that end needing nothing (stopped, or done without commits) only move the Live Activity.

| The job | Title | Body | `category` |
|---|---|---|---|
| Done with commits it couldn't publish by itself | `Couldn't publish: <title>` | `publishError` (else the summary's first line) | `ready` |
| `failed` | `Failed: <title>` | the reason (the summary's first line) | `job` |
| `interrupted` | `Interrupted: <title>` | the reason | `job` |
| Oby's review stopped asking for changes | `Changes requested: <title>` | `Oby's review found a blocker it asked to fix before. Take it from here.` | `job` |
| Its agent asked a question (`question`), which waits in the inbox | `Question: <title>` | the question | `job` |
| Its agent waits for the user's OK (`permission`), which waits in the inbox | `<permission title>?`, like `Run swift test?` | `<title>: <detail>` | `permission` |

```json
{"aps": {"alert": {"title": "Couldn't publish: Review oby #33", "body": "Couldn't push oby/review-33: the remote hung up."},
         "category": "ready", "sound": "default", "thread-id": "jobs"}, "job": "9f08fd10"}
```

- `job` is the job's `id`: tapping the notification opens it. The `ready` category offers **Publish** (it unlocks the phone and brings the app forward, where Face ID signs the approval as in the app) and **Open**; `job` offers **Open**; `permission` is for **Allow**, **Always allow** and **Deny** (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/permission` with the job's `permission.id`) and **Open**.
- `apns-collapse-id` is the job's `id`, so a newer state replaces the older notification.
- When a job stops needing you (published, from the iPhone or the Mac, dismissed, or running again after a message), the runner sends a background push (`apns-push-type: background`, priority 5), `{"aps": {"content-available": 1}, "clear": "<job id>"}`, and the app removes that job's notification. The app also removes them whenever it opens and sees the job needs nothing.
- The runner remembers what it last said for each job in `apns/notices.json`, so a restart doesn't say it again, and jobs a restart interrupted notify once it's back.

### `GET /v1/settings/permissions` and `PUT /v1/settings/permissions`

Settings › Permissions: `{"mode": "ask"}`, the mode new jobs get (`bypass` until changed on the Swift runner, `sandbox` on obid), kept in the runner's `oby.sqlite`. `PUT` answers with the setting; `422` for a mode that isn't one. Jobs already dispatched keep theirs.

### `GET /v1/permissions/rules` and `DELETE /v1/permissions/rules/{id}`

The saved rules, oldest first:

```json
[{"id": "r1a2b3c4d", "rule": "Bash(swift test:*)", "repo": "deskree-inc/oby", "decision": "allow", "created": "2026-10-11T09:12:00Z"}]
```

`decision` is `allow` or `deny`; deny beats allow. "Always" on a permission saves one. `DELETE` forgets one and answers with the rules left; `404` for a rule the runner doesn't know.

### `GET /v1/settings/models` and `PUT /v1/settings/models`

Each tool's model, routine effort and complex-job effort, as in `status.models`; Cursor has a model and no effort: `"cursor": {"model": "auto"}` (`auto` lets Cursor pick). `PUT` takes `cursor` optionally: older clients leave it out and the value is kept; it must be a model id (`422` for anything else, such as an option). `GET /v1/settings/models/options` lists the choices: Claude Code's models and efforts, the models the signed-in Codex account offers (from its own `model/list`) with the efforts each takes, and Cursor's models from `cursor-agent models` on the first signed-in Cursor account (`{"models": [{"id", "name"}], "efforts": []}`, no models while none is signed in). Defaults are Claude Code `xhigh` and Codex `high` for `complexEffort`; older clients that omit it keep the current value.

The chat's set_setting action uses those same choices. It changes one tool's model, routine effort or `complexEffort` (Cursor's model only: `effort` and `complex_effort` must be null for it), maxRunning (1 through 20), or an account's weekly reserve (lift true or false). For example, "complex jobs on Codex at xhigh" sets Codex's `complexEffort`. Swift checks every value against the briefing before the runner applies it; a rejected model or effort includes the available choices. Each action reply names the old and new value. A request that changes two tools is planned as two actions so the brain receives a fresh briefing between them.

```json
{"claude": {"models": [{"id": "claude-opus-5-5", "name": "Opus 5.5"}], "efforts": ["low", "medium", "high", "xhigh", "max"], "complexEfforts": ["low", "medium", "high", "xhigh", "max"]},
 "codex": {"models": [{"id": "gpt-6-sol", "name": "GPT-6-Sol", "efforts": ["low", "medium", "high", "xhigh", "max", "ultra"]}], "complexEfforts": ["low", "medium", "high", "xhigh", "max", "ultra"]},
 "cursor": {"models": [{"id": "auto", "name": "Auto"}, {"id": "gpt-5", "name": "GPT-5"}], "efforts": [], "complexEfforts": []}}
```

### `GET /v1/settings/jobs` and `PUT /v1/settings/jobs`

How many jobs run at once, as in `status.maxRunning`: `{"maxRunning": 6}`, 1 to 20 (anything else is `422`). The runner keeps it across restarts. Raising it starts waiting jobs at once; lowering it stops nothing, and new jobs wait until fewer than the new cap run.

### `POST /v1/speech?device=<UUID>&utterance=<UUID>&final=false&language=auto`

Transcribes one utterance on the runner's CPU using whisper.cpp, independently of the iPhone and of any agent. It uses the same owner gate as the other `/v1` endpoints. This is the speech service only; a Watch gateway can call it later.

The body is binary audio: signed 16-bit little-endian PCM, 16 kHz, mono (`Content-Type: audio/pcm` or `application/octet-stream`), or a complete RIFF/WAV chunk containing that same format (`audio/wav` or `audio/x-wav`). Send sequential chunks of at most ten seconds; each WAV request has its own header. The HTTP body limit is 324,096 bytes including WAV metadata. Audio for an utterance may total at most 60 seconds (1,920,000 PCM bytes).

- `device`: stable UUID chosen by the app for this device. This is a concurrency key, not authentication.
- `utterance`: a fresh UUID for each utterance. Reuse it for every chunk of that utterance.
- `final`: `false` by default. Every non-final call appends the chunk and transcribes **all** audio so far with multilingual `base-q5_1`. A `true` call appends its optional last chunk and transcribes all audio with `small-q5_1`, then discards the utterance. An empty final body is allowed after earlier audio. Partial text can change.
- `language`: `auto` (default, detection while transcribing), `en` or `pt`. Use the same language on every call. Transcription keeps the spoken language; it does not translate. With `auto`, partials keep detecting the language from all audio so far. After at least five seconds of partial audio, the final pass reuses the latest detected English or Portuguese language to avoid a second encoder pass. Final-only requests, shorter partials and other detected languages still use automatic detection.

Returns `{"utterance":"<UUID>","text":"…","final":false}` (or `true`). Wait for each answer before sending the next chunk. Accepted chunks append exactly once per received call; this endpoint does not deduplicate retries. After an uncertain network result, let the session expire before starting again.

Only one utterance per device is open or transcribing at once (`409` for an overlapping call, a different utterance or a changed language). One resident server per distinct model and 32 open utterances are allowed across devices. Each model accepts one inference at a time (`503` when busy). A busy `503` consumes none of the rejected chunk and preserves earlier audio and its idle deadline; retry the same chunk with the same utterance UUID. Fast and final models can work independently. Idle utterances are discarded after 120 seconds following their last partial answer. `400` means invalid parameters/audio; `413` means a body or utterance is too large. Exceeding the utterance limit discards it. `415` means unsupported content type. `501` clearly identifies a missing server/model or invalid thread count. `500` means transcription failed or timed out: audio is discarded and the client starts a fresh utterance. Each inference has a 120-second deadline; a timeout kills that model’s server to release its audio. The next request or five-second maintenance pass restarts it.

Audio stays in memory throughout: normalized WAV is posted as multipart `file` data to whisper-server's `/inference` endpoint, with `language`, `prompt`, `response_format=verbose_json`, `no_language_probabilities=true` and `no_context=true`. Language probabilities are disabled because upstream otherwise encodes the audio again for that response. The runner consumes only the text and detected language; its public response is unchanged. Neither Oby nor the server writes audio or transcript files (`--convert` is never enabled). Servers bind only to `127.0.0.1`, have a random request-path prefix, receive no agent/login environment, and run directly under the runner outside `Agents.sandboxed`. Their stdout/stderr go to `/dev/null`; audio and transcripts never reach logs or agents. Startup removes legacy crash leftovers from `OBY_HOME/whisper/audio`. HTTP uses a fresh, verified loopback socket for each request, without cookies, cache or proxies. No previous utterance's decoder context is carried into another request.

The runner starts both model servers before serving requests and keeps them resident, restarting exited servers during maintenance or on demand. Startup warms each server with one second of generated silence (not microphone audio) using the short encoder window, and discards the result. Readiness and warmup share a 120-second startup deadline. A missing installation leaves the other API routes usable and returns `501` for speech. Runner shutdown kills its servers; systemd's default control-group cleanup also covers a runner crash. If both model paths are identical, they share one server. Reserve the configured loopback ports for this runner; an occupied port causes readiness to fail. Before sending any request bytes (including warmup), the runner verifies that the connected peer socket belongs to its spawned child: `/proc/net/tcp` and the child’s descriptors on Linux, `lsof` on macOS. Audio travels over that same verified socket, with no redirects or automatic reconnects, so a different listener cannot receive it even if the port changes hands. If ownership cannot be verified, speech fails closed.

Run `OBY_HOME=/srv/nvme/oby Runner/scripts/setup-whisper.sh` once as the runner's owner on tetrix-lab. It requires git, CMake, a C++ compiler, curl and flock (Ubuntu: `git cmake build-essential curl util-linux`). It pins whisper.cpp v1.8.2, builds static release `whisper-cli` and `whisper-server` binaries optimized for this CPU with GPU backends off, and atomically downloads multilingual `base-q5_1`, `large-v3-turbo-q5_0` and `small-q5_1`. Repeated runs reuse the checkout/build and completed downloads; interrupted downloads are retried. Existing unquantized models are retained but unused by default. See the [upstream server](https://github.com/ggml-org/whisper.cpp/blob/v1.8.2/examples/server/server.cpp) and [model distribution](https://github.com/ggml-org/whisper.cpp/blob/v1.8.2/models/download-ggml-model.sh).

Short audio no longer pays for Whisper's default 30-second padding. Each request sets `audio_ctx` to `min(1500, max(600, (ceil(seconds) + 1) * 50))`: at least 12 seconds, a second beyond the audio, and at most the full 30-second encoder window. The 11-second sample uses 600 frames. Utterances of 30–60 seconds use the full window and whisper.cpp's successive-window processing; the 60-second input cap is unchanged.

Measured on tetrix-lab (16 CPU cores), 2026-10-11, with whisper.cpp v1.8.2's 11-second `samples/jfk.wav`, CPU-only, eight inference threads, `no_context=true`, no timestamps, and prompt `oby, tetrix`. Timing covers the loopback inference request through the parsed response, excluding startup/model loading. Models were resident and warmed with generated silence. Loads are one-minute averages recorded before each request; concurrent work changed between runs, so this is not a controlled comparison with the earlier load-13.6 CLI measurements (base-q5_1 4.3 s, unquantized turbo 27 s).

| Model / language | Encoder frames | Inference seconds | Load before each request |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| small-q5_1 / en | 600 | 1.710, 2.083, 1.632 | 28.12, 27.15, 27.15 |
| large-v3-turbo-q5_0 / en | 600 | 13.129, 12.826, 13.715 | 48.08, 49.90, 46.28 |
| small-q5_1 / auto | 600 | 9.764, 2.991 | 27.15, 40.65 |
| large-v3-turbo-q5_0 / auto | 600 | 25.900, 24.094 | 49.00, 53.18 |

These first measurements used the text-only JSON response. A repeat with the production verbose response and language probabilities disabled measured small-q5_1 at **27.008, 10.515, 11.136 s**, at loads **76.72, 77.19, 74.21**. The same production-response repeat measured quantized turbo at **13.181, 12.641, 12.174 s**, at loads **75.15, 70.89, 60.78**. The returned word sequence matched in the response-format checks. Earlier full-window (1500-frame) resident runs were also slow: small 47.923/63.658 s (loads 75.91/83.18), quantized turbo 50.006/36.528 s (loads 71.16/73.02). Reducing threads to one, two or four did not improve the best eight-thread result; retain eight by default.

**Default: small-q5_1.** It reached about two seconds on the warmed English sample; turbo remains too slow. Startup warmup, the smaller encoder window and reuse of a sufficiently long partial's detected language avoid repeated setup and detection work in the live speech path. This is not a two-second guarantee under heavy contention: the current machine sometimes ran at loads above 100, and even the small model missed the target badly. Final-only automatic detection is also slower. Use `en`/`pt` when the device knows its language, or send live partials; longer utterances and Portuguese recognition still need real Watch measurements.

The runner reads this configuration from its environment at startup (restart after changes):

| Variable | Default |
| --- | --- |
| `OBY_WHISPER_SERVER` | `OBY_HOME/whisper/bin/whisper-server` |
| `OBY_WHISPER_FAST_MODEL` | `OBY_HOME/whisper/models/ggml-base-q5_1.bin` |
| `OBY_WHISPER_FINAL_MODEL` | `OBY_HOME/whisper/models/ggml-small-q5_1.bin` |
| `OBY_WHISPER_THREADS` | `8` per model server, allowing two inferences to share tetrix-lab's 16 CPU cores |
| `OBY_WHISPER_FAST_PORT` | `8171` (loopback only) |
| `OBY_WHISPER_FINAL_PORT` | `8172` (loopback only) |
| `OBY_SPEECH_NAMES` | Unset: `oby`, `tetrix` and the short names of the runner's current `Desk.repos` list. Set a comma-separated list to override these names. Duplicates are removed and the initial prompt is capped at 512 UTF-8 bytes, including for overrides. |

No GPU is needed. The sample timings above do not replace checks with real Watch recordings.

### `GET /v1/watches`, `POST /v1/watches` and `DELETE /v1/watches/{id}`

The Apple Watches paired with the runner, for [the Watch API](#the-watch-api). On this API, so only the owner's iPhone over the tailnet reaches them.

`POST /v1/watches` pairs one. The Watch makes its key in its Secure Enclave and hands the public half to the iPhone over WatchConnectivity; the iPhone signs the pairing with its Face ID key and sends it with the Cloudflare Access service token it gives the Watch:

```json
{"name": "Wellington's Apple Watch", "publicKey": "BK9…",
 "accessClientId": "4a1c….access", "accessClientSecret": "9f2e…",
 "approval": {"device": "d7c1…", "signedAt": "2026-10-11T09:12:30Z", "signature": "MEUCIQ…"}}
```

- `publicKey`: the Watch's P-256 key, X9.63, base64. `approval.signature`: ECDSA P-256 with SHA-256 (DER, base64) by the registered phone's key (`POST /v1/devices`) over `"oby-pair-watch\n<publicKey>\n<signedAt>"`, within 2 minutes, once. Agents on the runner's machine reach this API as the owner, but they can't make that signature.
- The service token goes to the runner's vault (`watch-access-client-id`, `watch-access-client-token`), where no agent sees it and the secret scan learns it, so nothing outgoing carries it. A newer pairing replaces it. No answer or event carries it.
- It returns `201` with `{"id": "5e0f…", "url": "https://oby-watch.example.com"}`: the Watch's id and the Watch API's address, which the iPhone hands the Watch with the token. A key the runner knows keeps its id. `409` when the runner has no `watch.json` yet, `403` for an approval that doesn't pass, `422` for a key or token that isn't one.

`GET /v1/watches` lists them, `[{"id": "5e0f…", "name": "Wellington's Apple Watch", "registered": "2026-10-11T09:12:31Z"}]`, never a key or the token. `DELETE /v1/watches/{id}` removes one (the iPhone's Settings): its requests fail from then on and its push tokens are dropped; with the last Watch gone, the service token leaves the vault. `404` for a Watch the runner doesn't know.

## The Watch API

The Watch works without its iPhone. While the phone is in reach, the Watch goes through it as before (WatchConnectivity, `Shared/WatchWire.swift`); otherwise it calls this API itself, and goes back to the phone once it's in reach again. watchOS has no Tailscale or VPN, and only plain HTTPS through `URLSession` (no WebSockets), so:

- **Where:** the runner listens on a second address, `127.0.0.1:7778` (`OBY_WATCH_PORT`), which serves only the endpoints below. A Cloudflare Tunnel (`cloudflared`) publishes it at a hostname of the owner's, and a Cloudflare Access application on that hostname lets through only requests carrying its service token (`CF-Access-Client-Id` and `CF-Access-Client-Secret` headers, a Service Auth policy).
- **Set up** on the runner's machine, in `$OBY_HOME/watch.json`, read again whenever it changes:

  ```json
  {"url": "https://oby-watch.example.com", "team": "example",
   "audience": "4714c1358e65fe4b408ad6d432a5f878f08194bdb4752441fd56faefa9b2b6f2",
   "keys": [{"kid": "…", "kty": "RSA", "alg": "RS256", "n": "…", "e": "AQAB"}]}
  ```

  `url` is the tunnel's hostname, `team` the Zero Trust team name, `audience` the Access application's AUD tag, and `keys` the `keys` list from `https://<team>.cloudflareaccess.com/cdn-cgi/access/certs`. Cloudflare rotates those keys every six weeks: paste the new list in. Without a usable `watch.json`, every request is `503`.
- **Never** `Tailscale-User-Login`: it means nothing here. Every request needs both of these, or it's `401` with the reason:
  1. **`Cf-Access-Jwt-Assertion`**, which Access adds to every request it lets through: an RS256 JWT, checked against `keys` (by `kid`), with `audience` in `aud`, `iss` `https://<team>.cloudflareaccess.com`, and `exp`, `nbf` and `iat` current (a minute of clock difference allowed).
  2. **A signature from a paired Watch**, over the request:
     - `Oby-Watch`: the Watch's id from pairing.
     - `Oby-Time`: when it was signed, in seconds since 1970. At most 2 minutes behind the runner's clock and 5 seconds ahead of it.
     - `Oby-Nonce`: 16 to 64 letters, digits, `-` or `_`, new for every request. A nonce the runner has seen from that Watch is refused, so a replayed request fails, after a restart too: the runner keeps used nonces in `$OBY_HOME/watch-nonces.json` (0600) until their request is too old to get in. When it can't save or read that file, Watch requests are `503`.
     - `Oby-Signature`: ECDSA P-256 with SHA-256 (DER, base64), by the Watch's Secure Enclave key, over the UTF-8 bytes of `"oby-watch\n<METHOD>\n<path and query, as sent>\n<Oby-Time>\n<Oby-Nonce>\n<hex SHA-256 of the body>"` (the body's hash is that of no bytes for a `GET`).
- Answers and errors are as on the tailnet API, vault secrets taken out the same way.

The endpoints, each as on the tailnet API unless it says otherwise:

| Endpoint | |
|---|---|
| `GET /v1/jobs?limit=50` | The jobs. |
| `GET /v1/jobs/{id}` | One job. |
| `GET /v1/events` | The event stream. The Watch follows it only while Oby is on screen. |
| `POST /v1/plans` | What was dictated: the work it plans starts, and the answer carries the jobs. |
| `POST /v1/jobs` | A job described whole; a plan's draft from before #269 is `410`. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages` | A word to the job's agent. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/dismiss` | Dismiss. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/move` | Move to another tool, account or model. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/start` | Start now, a job waiting for its `startWhen`. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/permission` | Answer a waiting permission. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/mode` | Change a job's mode. |
| `GET /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` | The sentence publishing would carry out. |
| `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` | Publish, approved on the Watch (below). |
| `POST /v1/notifications` | The Watch's push tokens (below). |
| `POST /v1/speech` | A piece of a held recording (below). |

### `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` on the Watch API

The Watch approves with a turn of the crown, after showing the runner's sentence. `{"action": "Push oby/fix-ab12cd to deskree-inc/oby and open a pull request into dev."}`: the signed request is the approval (fresh, once, from a paired Watch's key), and `action` must be the runner's own sentence exactly, else `403`. Then Publish runs as for `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` above, and returns `{"url": …}`.

### `POST /v1/notifications` on the Watch API

The Watch's own push tokens: `{"token": "5b0e9c…", "topic": "com.deskree.oby.watchkitapp", "environment": "production", "kind": "alert"}`.

- `kind` `"alert"` (the default) is the Watch app's device token. The runner sends it exactly the alerts and clearing pushes it sends the iPhone (`POST /v1/notifications` above), the same payload with the same `apns-collapse-id`, so the Watch shows each alert once.
- `kind` `"widget"` is the complications' WidgetKit push token. When the number of jobs that need you or are working changes, the runner sends `{"aps": {"content-changed": true}}` (`apns-push-type: widgets`, topic `<topic>.push-type.widgets`), and the complications fetch the jobs themselves. A newer widget token replaces the Watch's older one.
- It returns `{"push": true}` or `{"push": false}`, and `422`, as `POST /v1/notifications` does. Unpairing the Watch drops its tokens.

### `POST /v1/speech?request={id}&sequence={n}` on the Watch API

A piece of the recording the user is holding the talk button for: the body is mono signed 16-bit little-endian PCM at 16 kHz (`application/octet-stream`), up to 32,000 bytes (one second), `request` the hold's id (8 to 64 letters, digits or `-`) and `sequence` 0, 1, 2… in order.

The Watch API doesn't pass pieces on to the runner's speech service (`POST /v1/speech` on the tailnet API, above) yet: a well-formed piece is answered `501`, `{"error": "Speech-to-text isn't set up on the runner yet. Dictate instead."}`, and the Watch stops recording and offers the system's dictation. `400` without `request` and `sequence`, `422` for an empty, odd or too-long piece.

## What the runner never does

- **Push to main:** not for anyone, not even with an approval.
- **Force-push, except a rebase of its own branch:** only an `oby/` branch the runner made, only over the commit it pushed there last (`--force-with-lease`), never `main` or `dev` ([`GET /v1/jobs/{id}/publish`](#get-v1jobsidpublish)).
- **Merge, except into `dev` after the Mac check:** the runner squash-merges a pull request it opened only into `dev`, only when Oby's review approved it, the Mac check passed on the same commit and the secret scan is clean (Mac check, above). Never into `main`.
- **Publish what didn't pass:** only a job that ended `done` (its check passed, or it has none), with a clean secret scan, is pushed and gets a pull request; by hand, only with a fresh device signature. No comment goes out: Oby's own review of its own pull request and the Mac check's results on it are check runs (an Apple check posts nothing), and the `oby-failure` issues its post-mortems file in Oby's own repository (Engine/Failures.swift) are updated in place, all after the secret scan; no agent posts to GitHub, and no GitHub review, approval or comment goes on anyone else's work.
- **Research jobs never commit or publish.** Proposed issues are filed only after the user taps to file them.
- **Touch the user's credentials:** it never reads, stores or forwards agent credentials, and each tool signs in through its own login on the runner.
- **Read outside text as instructions:** text from GitHub or other people reaches agents only as quoted files.

## obid only

The Rust daemon `obid` (`core/`, docs/ARCHITECTURE.md) serves this whole API, so the apps keep working against it unchanged, and also the endpoints in this section. **The Swift runner is frozen and never implements them.** They are what Obi-to-Obi work and the end-to-end scenario (e2e/SCENARIO.md) need. JSON keys are camelCase as everywhere in this API; objects of the Obi-to-Obi protocol (envelopes, agent cards, action bodies) appear exactly as the protocol defines them, in snake_case (obi-ai-bot/obi-sphere, `spec/`). None of these endpoints is on the gateway's allowlist (Runner/Routes.swift): they are managed on the machine itself. A phone answers an Obi-to-Obi request through the inbox endpoints it already has.

### obid only: what obid leaves out

`obid` serves every endpoint and key above, with one rule the Swift runner doesn't have: **it never merges a pull request**, for its owner or for anyone else (docs/ARCHITECTURE.md, "Trust boundaries"). Merging is a person's step. So, on `obid`:

- It never calls GitHub's merge endpoint (`PUT …/pulls/{n}/merge`) or merges any branch into another. A Mac check that passed is reported (its check run, `macCheck`), and nothing more happens until a person merges.
- A pull request that can't merge cleanly gets no rebase round by itself; the owner can ask for one like any other work.
- `mergeError` is always null, `mergeNeedsYou` always false, and `macCheck.tries` is never sent. No `merge` inbox item and no `Not merged:` alert is ever made.
- The `merged` pipeline stage is `upcoming` until `pullRequest.state` is `MERGED` (a person merged it), then `done`. Its `current` titles about merging (`Mac check passed · merging`, `Ready to merge`) read `Mac check passed · ready for you to merge` and `Ready for you to merge`.
- Deskree's promotion rules (Xcode Cloud waits, `dev` into `main`, the three-review escalation) are configuration that changes what `obid` checks and reports, never something that merges.
- Oby's review isn't run by `obid` yet (#525): `reviewing`, `review` and `reviewOf` are null, a published job's `reviewError` is `obid doesn't run Oby's review yet: a person reviews the pull request on GitHub.`, and its Review stage reads `Waiting for review · ` and that line until GitHub has a decision. No `Oby review` check run is written, and no Mac check is queued by a review.
- The Watch API's `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish` isn't served (#454).

### obid only: permissions answered without the owner (#586)

A job should never ask the owner about anything that stays inside its sandbox ("The job sandbox", under obid only: Obi-to-Obi review jobs: it writes only to its worktree and temporary folder, has no code-host login, no vault secret and a clone whose push URL is invalid). The owner's decision of 2026-10-17: the agent takes most decisions, and the owner is asked only when it is extremely necessary. So `obid` has a fifth permission mode, **`sandbox`**, and it is the default (Settings › Permissions, `GET /v1/settings/permissions`; a `permissions.json` with no saved mode means `sandbox`; a mode the owner saved stays). The Swift runner doesn't have it and its default stays `bypass`.

| In `sandbox` | |
|---|---|
| **Answered by obid's rules, recorded on the job** | File edits in the worktree; commands and tests (`cargo test`, `npm test`, `git commit`); reads and searches inside the worktree; the agent's own bookkeeping (its to-do list, a helper agent) and the MCP tools of enabled plug-ins, as in every mode. |
| **Refused** (the fixed rules, in every mode) | Pushing or posting (only Publish does), a path outside the worktree, a background subagent, other MCP tools. The agent hears why. |
| **Asked** (a `permission` on the job and a `permission` inbox item, the card saying `why`) | The network beyond the model's API: a web fetch or search, a download or an install (`curl`, `wget`, `ssh`, `npm install`, `cargo add`, `pip install`, `python3 -m pip`, `git clone`, `git fetch`, `docker pull`, a bare `yarn` or `bundle`, `go mod`, `pipx`, `uvx`, `deno`, `gradle`, `mvn`, `rustup`, `swift package resolve`, `npx` or `bunx` of a tool the repository didn't install, …), a service's client (`psql`, `redis-cli`, `kubectl`, `aws`, `gcloud`, `terraform`, …), bash's `/dev/tcp` and `/dev/udp`, and what obid can't read: a program named by a variable or a substitution (`$c`, `$(echo curl)`), inline code (`python3 -c`, `node -e`, `perl -e`, `ruby -e`) and `find -exec`. Secrets, logins and the vault: a well-known secret file or folder by its name (`~/.ssh`, `.aws`, `.kube`, `.docker`, `api-token`, `auth.json`, `.credentials.json`, `.netrc`, `.npmrc`, `.env` but not `.env.example`, and a glob that could match one, like `.cred*`), the agent's own login (the job's real account folder, its login copy and the links to it in the job's `HOME`, and `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `$CODEX_HOME`, `$CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`), the tokens obid gives a job (`$OBY_MCP_TOKEN`, `$OBY_CHECK_TOKEN`), printing the environment (`printenv`, a bare `env` or `set`, `export -p`, `/proc/*/environ`), a keychain's passwords (`security find-…`), `gh auth`, or a value the vault keeps, in any form, anywhere in the call. A committed `.env.test` or `.env.development` in the worktree (git has it) is read without asking. A read or search outside the worktree and the job's temporary folder, and a command that names a file outside them and the system's folders (`/usr`, `/etc`, `/opt`, `/dev`, `/System`, `/Library`, …), like `cat /Users/someone/notes.txt` or `ls /tmp`. A tool obid doesn't know stays inside the sandbox, and a Cursor call that gives only a title for its command, or no place for its read. |

- **The network questions are best-effort until the OS limits egress.** Neither job sandbox limits an agent's network today: both give it the network so it reaches its model's API (on Linux slirp4netns, which also reaches the host's loopback at `10.0.2.2`; on macOS the profile denies no network). So what reaches the network beyond the model's API is caught only by reading the command, and a program that opens a socket itself isn't caught. OS-level egress filtering is [#616](https://github.com/obi-ai-bot/obi/issues/616).
- Matching a command's words is best-effort, like publishing's (obid only: publishing). `python -c` counts as inline code only before `-m` or a script (`python3 -m pytest -c setup.cfg` doesn't), and `find -exec` reaches the network only when what it runs does (`-exec rm {} +` doesn't). obid reads a command the way a shell does: each simple command between `|`, `;`, `&`, `(`, `)` and new lines outside quotes, through the wrappers before it (`sudo`, `timeout 5s`, `env FOO=1`), and the code inside it too: `$(…)` and backticks (also inside double quotes), what `bash -c`, `sh -ec` or `eval` runs, and a here-document or here-string fed to a shell. A quoted argument, any other here-document, a commit's message (`git commit -m`) and the pattern of `grep` or `rg` are words, not commands or files, so a commit whose message says "curl" or "~/.ssh" goes through. Code nested more than four levels deep asks, as a secret does. A script that opens a socket itself isn't caught. The OS sandbox is what confines the files; the network is not confined for an agent (it reaches its model's API through slirp4netns), which is why a command's network use is asked about, not answered.
- **Secrets wait for the owner in every mode** (the re-review of #586). What touches secrets, a login or the vault asks in `ask`, `acceptEdits`, `plan` and `bypass` too, reads included, and no mode or saved rule lets it through: only the owner's own answer to its card does (the brain can't, even once). A waiting secrets card is never settled by a change of mode or a new rule, so neither the brain's `set_mode` nor `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/mode` answers it. This narrows `bypass` on obid: it answers everything but secrets.
- **Paths are read where the command runs.** A relative word is a path from the worktree, or from where a `cd` (or `pushd`) earlier in the same command line leaves it, with links followed: a committed link to the account's folder (`cat link/settings.json`) is a secret, and `cat ../../x` is outside. A subshell's `cd` (`( … )`, `$(…)`, backticks) ends with the subshell: after `(cd sub)` the folder is the one before. A command's own folder is read like a `cd` for that command: `git -C <dir>` (before git's subcommand), `make -C <dir>` or `--directory=<dir>`, `tar -C <dir>`, `env -C <dir>` or `--chdir=<dir>`; it is outside like any path, and its relative words are paths from there. A folder counts as a system folder only when it is one both as written and after links (on macOS `/home` leads into `/System`). A `cd` from an earlier call isn't known, so `../docs` after a previous call's `cd core` asks.
- **A folder obid can't tell makes the rest of the line `Outside`** (#636). When obid can't tell where a `cd` lands, every later simple command of the same line is outside, whatever it names: `cd -`, `cd $OLDPWD` or any folder named by a variable or a substitution (`cd "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)"`), `popd`, `pushd` with no folder or a place in its stack (`+1`), a bare `cd` when obid doesn't know the job's `HOME`, a `cd` inside code obid reads out of a word (`bash -c`, `eval`, a here-document fed to a shell), and a `cd` in a pipeline or in the background (a shell may run it in a subshell of its own). Likewise a command whose own folder (`git -C "$D"`, above) obid can't tell is outside. The commands before it aren't.
- **git settings that run a program ask** (`Unknown`). A command may give git exactly these, as written, and nothing else: the variables `GIT_EDITOR=true`, `GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR=:`, `GIT_PAGER=cat` and `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` (before the program, or after `env`), and `git -c core.editor=true` and `git -c commit.gpgsign=false` before git's subcommand (#636). Each keeps git from opening an editor, a pager, a prompt or a signing program. Who a commit is by and when (`GIT_AUTHOR_*`, `GIT_COMMITTER_*`, `GIT_PAGER_IN_USE`, `-c user.name=…`, `-c user.email=…`) and colours (`-c color.*`) go through whatever their value: they run no program. Any other `GIT_*` variable (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `GIT_PAGER=less`, `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS`) and any other `-c` setting (`core.sshCommand`, `core.pager`, `alias.*`, `gpg.program`) ask, as do `--config-env` and `--exec-path=`. `busybox` and `toybox` count as network, like the other multi-call tools.
- **Known gaps, under #616**: programs that run code they're given without a flag obid reads, like `awk` (its `system()`), `lua` and `Rscript`, aren't treated as network: asking for every `awk` would block normal work. And these ask though they're harmless, by design: a write to `/tmp` (outside the job's folders; the sandbox refuses it anyway), a `sed` whose text names `GITHUB_TOKEN`, and `"$PYTHON" -m pytest` (a program named by a variable). `/Library` stays readable, like the other system folders.
- **The owner's rules still apply**, in this order: a deny rule refuses; a saved allow rule lets through what would ask for the network or a read outside the worktree (the owner's own earlier decision), but **never secrets**: a card about secrets has `rule` and `always` null, so it can only be allowed once. "Always" covers what was asked about, not the whole tool: a fetch saves `WebFetch(domain:<site>)` (that site and its subdomains), a search outside the worktree `Grep(<place>)` (likewise `Glob`, `LS`). A job whose `mode` this obid can't read (one a newer obid wrote) is treated as `ask`; a job from before modes is still `bypass`. Then `sandbox` answers or asks as above. In `plan` and the other modes nothing changes.
- **Standing rules stay confirm cards** (the owner's decision of 2026-10-16). `sandbox` is not a standing rule the brain can create: the brain's plan can't name it (`set_mode` and a dispatch's `mode` take `ask`, `acceptEdits`, `plan`, `bypass`), `bypass`, "always allow" and accepting another Obi's request are `confirm` items as before (obid only: the brain), and the agent's own questions (#448) are asked as before. Only the owner's API calls (`PUT /v1/settings/permissions`, `POST /v1/jobs/{id}/mode`) set `sandbox`.
- **The owner can go back to asking for commands**: set Settings › Permissions to `acceptEdits` (edits go, every command asks) or `ask`. Jobs already dispatched keep their mode.
- **The record.** Every permission obid answers itself in a job of the owner's own, the refused ones too, is kept on the job: `answered`, the latest 50, oldest first, and `answeredCount`, how many obid answered in all (the agent's bookkeeping calls, `TodoWrite`, `Task` and `Agent`, aren't counted; what the owner answers themselves isn't obid's). A waiting prompt that a new mode or a new rule answers is counted when it is answered.

  ```json
  {"answered": [{"id": "p1a2b3", "tool": "Bash", "title": "Run cargo test", "detail": "cargo test --workspace",
                 "decision": "allowed", "at": "2026-10-17T09:12:00Z"},
                {"id": "p4d5e6", "tool": "Bash", "title": "Run git push", "detail": "git push origin HEAD",
                 "decision": "refused", "reason": "obid publishes the job's work itself. …", "at": "2026-10-17T09:12:03Z"}],
   "answeredCount": 2}
  ```

  `decision` is `allowed` or `refused`; `reason` is what the agent heard, only on a refusal; `detail` is cut to 300 characters, with a login in an address (`https://user:pass@host`) and every word that looks like a token (the secret scan's rules) taken out. The `permission` a job waits on may also carry `why`, one sentence saying why it asks when `sandbox` would otherwise have answered ("It reaches the network beyond the model's API.", "It touches secrets or the vault.", "It reads outside the job's worktree."). The Swift runner sends neither key.
- Acceptance, as the API tests show it: a job that edits files, runs tests and installs nothing ends `done` with no inbox item and `answered` listing each; one that tries to push is refused and says so in `answered`; one that installs, fetches a page, reads `~/.ssh` or lists `/etc` waits with a `permission` and `why`.

### obid only: publishing the owner's work

`obid` publishes as [Job](#job) and [`GET`](#get-v1jobsidpublish) and [`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/publish`](#post-v1jobsidpublish) say, by itself when a job ends `done` with commits made in that run, and by hand with a registered device's signature. What is particular to `obid`:

- **Where it runs.** Every git command of publishing runs in `obid`'s own clone (`home/clones/<owner>/<name>`), on the commit the job's branch is at there (`obid` took the job's commits into the clone and moved the branch), never in the job's worktree, whose git files the agent could write. Objects are read as they are (`--no-replace-objects`), no hook runs, and every diff the scan reads is `--no-ext-diff --no-textconv --text`, root commits included (`--root`). What the scan reads, it reads with the clone's own configuration only (`GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1`, `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null`): a setting of the person's (`log.showRoot = false`, an attributes file) can't narrow it. The fetch and the push keep the person's git configuration (their remotes' addresses and sign-in).
- **Where it goes.** The push goes to the repository's `remote` (obid only: configuration) by its address, not to `origin`, whose push URL stays invalid; it pushes one commit, `--no-follow-tags --no-signed --recurse-submodules=no`, and `--force-with-lease` only for a rebase of its own `oby/` branch over the commit it pushed there last. A remote whose address carries a login (`https://user:token@…`) is refused (`publishError` says so), since the address would be in git's arguments. For an `https` remote, git signs in with the owner's `gh` as its credential helper (`gh auth git-credential`: the credential travels on a pipe, never in an argument or the environment). Clones and fetches of an `https` remote sign in the same way, git's own helpers reset first, so a private repository clones with nothing set up but gh's own sign-in (no `gh auth setup-git`), and gh's token never reaches another helper (after a clone git asks every helper to `store` it, and Apple's git turns `osxkeychain` on system-wide). Without a gh, a clone uses git's own settings.
- **GitHub.** `obid` has no GitHub app: the pull request is opened (`gh pr create`, ready for review, never a draft), found (`gh pr list --head`), brought up to date (`gh pr edit --body-file`) and followed (`gh pr view`) with the owner's own `gh` login: the `gh` Oby.app carries (`Contents/MacOS/gh`, pinned and signed with the app, apps/mac/app/Tools/bundle-gh.sh) when obid runs from inside the app, else the one on the login shell's `PATH` (`GET /v1/status` says `"github": "owner"` and `"checks": "no app"`). `gh` runs with `obid`'s environment and `GH_PROMPT_DISABLED=1`; its description goes in a 0600 file in `home/tmp/publish-<job id>/`, removed afterwards. Only a pull request URL of the job's own repository, `https://github.com/<repo>/pull/<n>`, is kept or followed. An open pull request is taken as the job's only when it comes from the repository itself (`isCrossRepository` false: a fork's branch of the same name is someone else's), into the job's base, and its head is the commit obid just pushed, or the one it pushed before; otherwise obid opens its own. Without `gh`, `publishError` says the pull request couldn't be opened; a push that went out before (one to an `ssh` or `file` remote needs no `gh`) is listed in `outgoing` and counted in `pushed`. Only a remote on GitHub (`https://github.com/…`, `ssh://git@github.com/…` or `git@github.com:…`) gets a pull request, unless the repository's `pullRequests` says otherwise: for any other, nothing is asked of `gh` (no pull request, no issue read), and the push is the whole publish (#627). `POST` then answers `{"url": null}`.
- **The base** is fetched from the remote first and pinned to the commit it is at then: what the push sends beyond it is what the scan reads. Jobs on one repository that end together all publish (#628): obid's clone sets `fetch.hideRefs` to `worktrees/`, so a fetch doesn't check what it got against the HEAD of another job's worktree, whose commits stay in that job's own object folder until obid keeps them (git said `did not send all necessary objects` otherwise); one fetch runs at a time per clone, no job's commits land in the clone while one runs, and a fetch that still gets that answer is tried once more. When it can't be fetched, nothing goes out (`publishError` `Not published: couldn't fetch <base>: …`, git's own line, never its command line or the remote's address).
- **The scan** is `crate::scan`'s, on everything that goes out: each commit's patch (`git log -p --diff-merges=remerge`), its added lines (named by the diff's `+++` line, which names a file exactly), with gitleaks on them; every name a commit adds at a path its parent didn't have (each file's, folder's and submodule's own name, from `git log --raw -z --diff-merges=separate --ignore-submodules=none`, so never split or quoted, a merge read against each parent and a submodule never hidden, whatever the clone's settings; a name already on the base at the same path is the base's), with the built-in rules reading a name as they read a line, and gitleaks on them, one name per line (a newline in a name written as `\n`), a finding it can't place in a name counting all the same; a file whose name alone holds secrets (`.env`, a private key's file) counts only as a file, not a folder named so; every file those commits send, read whole, for the secrets `obid` holds (its API token, its Obi key, the Sphere's delegation and token, and the vault's: [obid only: the vault](#obid-only-the-vault)), in every form `crate::secrets` knows, a file over 32 MiB being a finding; each commit as git keeps it (`cat-file`), its author and committer (`commit author`) and its message's own bytes (`commit message`); and the branch's name and the pull request's title and description. A commit whose headers aren't exactly what git writes for a plain commit (one `tree`, its `parent`s, one `author`, one `committer`, in UTF-8: no `encoding`, which would re-encode its message, no signature, no other header) or whose message isn't UTF-8 text is refused: nothing goes out, and `publishError` names the commit, never what it holds. A name that holds a possible secret is never repeated: wherever `obid` says where it found something (`publishError`, the summary, the log, the API), that file is `a file whose name obid won't show`, and the line adds that it is among the names of the files and folders the branch adds to its base, which is where the owner looks. A possible secret in `commit author`, or in a file obid won't name, gets no fix round. A file finding names the commit whose patch introduced it, and whether the branch's tip still has it. A gitleaks that is installed and can't run is a scan that couldn't run: nothing goes out, and the job stays `done` with `publishError` `Not published: the secret scan couldn't run (…)`. gitleaks always reads a fixed first line of obid's (`obid`, no word a rule looks for) before the lines it's given: what starts like a PDF, a zip or a gzip (`%PDF-1.4`, `PK\x03\x04`) is something gitleaks skips whole, so a diff whose first added line looked like one would otherwise be scanned as nothing. A finding wholly on that line doesn't count; one that runs past it (its `EndLine` 2 or more) does, on the first line given. gitleaks also sniffs further in (`ustar` at byte 257 for a tar, `CD001` at byte 32769 for an ISO image) and then reads nothing at all, whatever comes first, so obid puts a canary last: a made-up GitHub token, new every run and never kept, in the shape gitleaks' `github-pat` rule always flags, after another `obid` line, so a last line that only names a secret (`API_KEY=`, `token:`) isn't read on into it. A report without the canary (gitleaks exited 0, or wrote no report it could read) is a scan that couldn't run, `gitleaks didn't read its input`, and nothing goes out; the canary's own finding, and any wholly on that `obid` line, are dropped. gitleaks runs with `--ignore-gitleaks-allow`: a `gitleaks:allow` comment in what's scanned hides nothing (without it, gitleaks drops every finding in the part of its input that holds one, and past about 300 KB the canary is in another part, so the scan would read clean). The Swift engine's own gitleaks call has none of this yet (#657).
- **The pull request's description** ends with `by Oby: Oby opened this pull request after the check passed and the secret scan was clean. Oby never merges it: a person does.` in place of the Swift runner's merge sentence.
- **Following** reads a published pull request when `obid` starts, right after it publishes one, and every three minutes while it is open; a read or a try in the last three minutes isn't repeated. While it is open and GitHub's decision is `APPROVED`, the Merged stage is `current` with `Ready for you to merge`.
- **By hand.** The device's key is the one it registered (`POST /v1/devices`); each approval is let through once, and what was used is kept for 10 minutes in `home/approvals.json` (0600), so a restart opens no replay. An approval is used up when it is checked, even when publishing then fails. The sentence names the branch, not its commits, so an approval publishes only the run that was approved: when the job's latest run ended after the device signed (`signedAt`), or when `obid` described an earlier run of it (`GET`) in the two minutes before the device signed, the approval could be of either, and `POST` answers `409` `The job ran again since obid said what publishing it would do. Approve it again in two minutes.` What `obid` described, and when, is kept with the job, across restarts. This is a narrowing, not a seal: signing the commit itself is #553. While a publish by hand runs, the job takes no words (`POST /v1/jobs/{id}/messages`, `422`) and can't be dismissed (`409`). The refusals are `403`: `That device isn't registered with this runner.`, `The signature doesn't match the device's key.`, `The device approved something else. Approve it again.`, `The approval is more than two minutes old. Approve it again.` and `That approval was already used. Approve it again.`. A publish that fails answers `409` with its line.
- **The journal.** `obid`'s log has one line per thing it sent (`job <id>: sent push <repo> <branch> <commit>`), ids only, and one line per publish that didn't go out, saying why (never a secret).

### obid only: failures

`obid` keeps [Failure records](#failure-record) and serves [`GET /v1/failures`](#get-v1failureslimit50), [`GET /v1/failures/{id}`](#get-v1failuresid) and [`POST /v1/failures/{id}/fix`](#post-v1failuresidfix) with every key above (#445). What is particular to `obid`:

- **What it records.** Each time a job changes, `obid` looks at it as the Swift runner does (Engine/Failures.swift, `found`): a `failed` job is `limit` when it stopped at a limit, `publishError` when its summary starts `Not published:`, and `failed` otherwise; an `interrupted` one, which needs you, is `limit` when it stopped at a limit, `publishError` when its `reason` starts `Not published:`, and `failed` otherwise; a `done` job with `publishError` set is `publishError`. Only while it is news (it ended within a day), and once: the record's id is `<job>-<trigger>-<when it ended, in seconds>`, and a record is never written again. `obid` looks only as a job changes: it has no sweep, and doesn't look at ended jobs again when it starts, so a failure is missed if `obid` stops between keeping the job and writing its record (#571). A job the owner stopped isn't a failure, and a `done` one with a `reason` (nothing committed, a review that committed) isn't either. The other triggers need what `obid` doesn't run (Oby's review, merging, the minute's sweep, the brain's fallback) and are never recorded: `reviewCap`, `noVerdict`, `noReviewer`, `checkError`, `unnamedFailure`, `mergeError`, `flakyTest`, `stuck`, `brainFallback`, and the interventions `handMerge` and `outsideCommit` (a person merging is how `obid` works, never an intervention). `intervention` is always false.
- **Where.** One file per record, `home/failures/<id>.json` (0600, in a 0700 folder), never pruned. `GET /v1/failures/{id}` takes only ids `obid` makes (letters, digits and dashes): anything else is `404`, like an id it doesn't have (`There's no failure <id>.`). `limit` is a whole number (`422` `limit is a number.` otherwise, as `GET /v1/jobs`), below 0 is none and above 1,000 is 1,000.
- **The evidence**, with every secret `obid` knows taken out (its API token, its Obi key, the Sphere's delegation and token, and the vault's once it is ported, #457: `crate::secrets`), and so are `title` and `what` (a limit names the check's command): `summary` is the end of the job's report (4,000 characters), `step` its stage, phase, step and round, `check` the end of what its last check printed (`home/logs/<job>/check.log`, 3,000 characters) and `Publish: <publishError>`, or null; a `check.log` written before a failure of the job `obid` recorded already is an earlier run's and isn't quoted. `reviews` and `log` are always empty (`obid` runs no review of its own, and keeps no log lines in memory), `worktree` and `source` always null (both are read at the post-mortem).
- **No post-mortem yet.** `obid` runs no post-mortem turn, so `postMortem`, `studyError`, `issue`, `issueError`, `fix`, `fixedBy` and `fixedAt` are null, `tries` is 0 and `acted` false. With nothing studied, nothing is acted on: no `What went wrong:` paragraph in the job's report, no `oby-failure` issue filed, updated or closed on GitHub, no `failure` inbox item, and `POST /v1/failures/{id}/fix` answers `409` `Oby hasn't studied this failure yet.` for every record it has (`404` for one it doesn't). When the post-mortem comes, filing its issue is an outgoing action: it goes through the publish gate (the secret scan on the title, the body and the arguments; nothing goes out if the scanner can't run) with the owner's `gh`, as publishing does. The post-mortem, acting on it and the fix job are #571.

### obid only: configuration

```sh
obid serve --config /path/to/config.json
```

```json
{"listen": "127.0.0.1:7811",
 "home": "/abs/path/obid-home",
 "owner": "wellington",
 "agents": {"claude": {"bin": "claude", "accounts": [{"id": "default", "name": "Default", "configDir": null}]}},
 "repos": [{"repo": "obi-e2e/fixture-app", "remote": "file:///abs/path/remote.git", "defaultBranch": "main", "check": "node --test"}],
 "allowFileRemotes": false,
 "bundledPlugins": "/abs/path/obi/plugins",
 "web": "/abs/path/obi/apps/web/dist",
 "telemetry": false}
```

- `listen`: an address on 127.0.0.1. obid binds only that, and refuses with `403` every request whose `Host` isn't `127.0.0.1`, `localhost` or `[::1]` (with or without a port), as a Mac runner does. Required, but on macOS, where it is `127.0.0.1:7777` when left out: where the Mac app looks for the runner on the same Mac.
- `home` (required, absolute): everything obid keeps, and the only place it writes besides its `HOME` and `TMPDIR`: its database, the API token (`api-token`, 0600), the accounts the API changed (`accounts.json`, 0600, and the folders it makes, `accounts/<id>`), the Obi key (`keys/`, 0600), clones (`clones/<owner>/<name>`), job worktrees (`worktrees/<job id>`), plug-ins (`plugins/`, `plugin-data/`), and the settings the API changes: `settings.json` (`maxRunning`, `PUT /v1/settings/jobs`) and `permissions.json` (the permission settings and the saved rules: `PUT /v1/settings/permissions`, `/v1/permissions/rules`), each kept across restarts.
- `owner` (required): the owner's id, as their Sphere knows them.
- `agents.claude.bin`: the Claude Code adapter's binary, a path or a command name found on the login shell's `PATH` (AGENTS.md, "PATH"). `accounts`: each `{id, name, configDir, payer}` (`payer` optional); they are the seed the accounts API changes ([obid only: accounts](#obid-only-accounts)); `configDir` is the account's folder, where its login is kept (and, on macOS, what names its Keychain item), or null for the tool's own default login; no box ever gets the folder itself: every run gets a folder of its own with a copy of the login ("The job sandbox", #655). `GET /v1/status` sends each one with `label: null` and `signedIn` true when its login file is there (#626): Claude Code's `.credentials.json` in its `configDir`, or `~/.claude/.credentials.json` when `configDir` is null; Codex's `auth.json` in its `configDir`, or `~/.codex/auth.json`. obid only looks whether the file exists (a regular file, not a link), never at what it holds. A Claude Code login only in the macOS Keychain counts when `claudeKeychain` is on, since obid then copies it where a run reads it (The job sandbox, under obid only: Obi-to-Obi review jobs); with it off it doesn't, since the job sandbox denies the Keychain. Cursor's accounts stay `signedIn: true`: a job whose tool says the login is missing fails saying so. Routing (a job with no `account`, the brain's dispatches and reviews) takes the first signed-in account of the tools in order (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor), so with `agent` left out a job goes to Codex when no Claude Code account is signed in; when none is, it takes the first account as before, and the tool's own words say why it can't run. A job naming its account runs there. Settings › Connectors in the web app shows such an account as not checked, never as connected. Codex and Cursor take the same shape when their adapters land (`CODEX_HOME`, `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`).
- `repos`: the repositories jobs may work in. `remote` is an `https://` or `ssh` URL, or a `file://` URL only when `allowFileRemotes` is true (the e2e test's fixture). `check` is the one-line command that decides `tests_pass` and serves as the job's check. `pullRequests` (optional): whether its pushes get a pull request on GitHub with the owner's `gh`; left out, only when `remote` is GitHub's (obid only: publishing, "GitHub"). obid's own tests set it for the fixture's `file://` remote, with a stand-in `gh` (#627).
- `bundledPlugins` (absolute): the folder of the plug-ins obid ships, one `<id>/` folder each with its `obi-plugin.json` (this repository's `plugins/`). At every start, before the plug-in host starts, obid copies each of them into `home/plugins/<id>` when `home/plugins/<id>` is missing (regular files and folders only, through a temporary folder renamed into place), and installs it **disabled**: nothing is written to `home/plugins.json`. A folder already in `home/plugins/` is left as it is, and so is whether the owner enabled it. Left out, it is the `plugins/` folder of the repository obid was built from (`core/crates/obid/../../../plugins`, fixed when obid is compiled); when that folder isn't there (an obid copied elsewhere), obid installs nothing, logs why, and runs as usual.
- `web` (absolute): the built web app obid serves at `/app/` ([obid only: the web app](#obid-only-the-web-app)), `apps/web/dist` after `npm run build` in `apps/web`. Left out, it is that folder of the repository obid was built from (`core/crates/obid/../../../apps/web/dist`, fixed when obid is compiled); when it holds no `index.html`, `/app/` answers `404` saying the web app isn't built.
- `telemetry`: false unless the owner turns it on. obid sends nothing to anyone otherwise.
- `vault` (optional): where the owner's vault is ([obid only: the vault](#obid-only-the-vault)): `{"store": "file"}` for `home/vault/secrets.json`, or on macOS `{"store": "keychain"}` for the Swift runner's Keychain items, with an optional `"service"` in place of `com.deskree.oby.runner.vault` (tests use a throwaway one). Left out, it is the Keychain when `home` is the Mac app's runner home on macOS (compared with links resolved and letter case ignored), and the file otherwise. `"keychain"` anywhere but macOS is refused, and so is a blank `service` or one with spaces around it.
- `turnBudget`: how long one conversation turn may plan and act, in seconds, 1 to 110; 110 when left out (obid only: the brain).
- `stallAfter` (obid only): how long a job may go without progress before it stalls (`limit`, above), in seconds, 1 to 86,400; 900 when left out.
- `jobCeilingHours` (obid only): the safety ceiling of a job's running time, every run added up, against runaway cost only (`limit`, above), in hours, 1 to 168; 12 when left out.
- `jobTokenBudget` (obid only): the safety budget of a job's tokens, cache reads left out (`limit`, above), 1 to 10,000,000,000; 50,000,000 when left out.
- `leastThink`: for tests. The least time a brain is asked to think in, in seconds, from 1 to less than `turnBudget`; 1 when left out. With that much of the turn's budget left or less, no brain is asked (`budget_spent`, #615). Tests widen it so a busy machine can't miss the window (#763).
- `orchestratorDelay`: how long the event orchestrator waits for nearby events to come together before its turn, in seconds, 1 to 600; 30 when left out (obid only: the brain, "The event orchestrator").
- `gateway`: where paired phones reach obid, an address on `127.0.0.1` like `127.0.0.1:8810`; no gateway when left out (obid only: the gateway).
- `claudeKeychain`: `true` lets obid copy Claude Code's login from the macOS Keychain where a boxed run reads it, only what calls the API, and have the Mac's own Claude Code refresh the default login in its last 5 minutes ("The job sandbox", below); `false` keeps it off. Left out, obid decides when it starts (the owner's decision of 2026-10-18): on macOS it turns it on when Claude Code's default login is in the Keychain and no `~/.claude/.credentials.json` is there (it asks only whether the item exists, never for what it holds), and off otherwise, so a fresh Mac's Claude accounts don't read as signed out. `GET /v1/status` says which: `"claudeKeychain": {"on": true, "auto": true}`, `auto` false when the configuration set it. Only on macOS; elsewhere it does nothing (`on` false). obid reads the Keychain with `/usr/bin/security`; its own tests set `OBID_SECURITY_BIN` to an absolute path of a stand-in, so a test's obid never reads the person's real Keychain.
- An account's `configDir` (any tool) is never one that holds obid's `home`, nor anywhere inside `home` but a folder of `home/accounts` (where the accounts API makes them): obid's home holds its database, clones and the jobs' worktrees.
- `conversation` (optional): how conversations run (obid only: the conversation harness). `agent`: `"claude"` (each conversation is a Claude Code session, the default), `"codex"` (each is a Codex thread) or `"plan"` (obid's plan loop answers, as before). `model`: the agent's model for conversations, `claude-sonnet-5-5` on Claude Code and Codex's own on Codex when left out. `budget`: how long one turn may take, in seconds, 10 to 3,600; 600 when left out. `turnBudget` stays the plan loop's.

**What Settings changes** is kept in `home` as files, not in the database: the permission rules and Settings › Permissions' default mode (`GET`/`PUT /v1/settings/permissions`, `/v1/permissions/rules`) in `home/permissions.json`, and `maxRunning` (`PUT /v1/settings/jobs`) in `home/settings.json`, both 0600 and kept across restarts.

The API token is the one in `home/api-token` (0600, at least 32 visible characters): obid makes it on first start, or takes the one put there before it starts. The shells on the same machine read it there. It is never in obid's environment or arguments, where any process of the same user can read it (on macOS through `KERN_PROCARGS2`, which no sandbox holds back; #439): obid refuses to start when `OBID_API_TOKEN` is set, saying to put the token in that file instead. The same holds for every secret obid holds: none is ever in its environment or arguments, nor in a child's. Every request sends `Authorization: Bearer <token>`; anything else is `401`. The one exception is reading the web app's own files under `/app/`, which hold no data ([obid only: the web app](#obid-only-the-web-app)); nothing under `/v1` takes anything but the token. Every `/v1` answer carries `Cache-Control: no-store` (unless it sets its own, like the event stream's `no-cache`) and `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`. `GET /v1/status` adds `"engine": "obid"` to `runner`. On SIGTERM or SIGINT obid stops taking requests, leaves its jobs as a restart would (After any restart, above) and exits 0 within 10 seconds.

**The Mac app on the same Mac (#440).** For Settings › Runner › This Mac, set obid's `home` to
`~/Library/Application Support/Oby/Runner` (expanded to an absolute path in the JSON) and leave
`listen` out, or use `127.0.0.1:7777`. This is `LocalRunner.home`, also where the app opens device
pairing. The app reads `api-token` there directly: no token in a process's environment or
arguments, no copy in UserDefaults, and no change to how the app starts. The file must be a
regular file owned by the current user with no group or other permissions (normally 0600),
at most 4096 bytes, and contain at least 32 visible ASCII characters with only optional trailing
CR/LF. Links, directories and malformed or exposed files are ignored. The existing Swift runner
continues using its keychain item (`com.deskree.oby.runner`, account `token`); that credential is
the fallback when no usable obid file exists or the server refuses its token with 401. A refused
credential is refreshed, so a stale file does not prevent connecting to a running Swift runner,
and rotating obid's token does not require restarting the app. Other obid homes and ports remain
valid for other clients; This Mac mode uses the fixed home and address above.

**Installing it (#585).** `install/install.sh` makes `home` (0700) and `home/api-token` (0600), writes this configuration (or keeps one already there, and then uses its `home` and `listen`), and runs obid as a launchd agent (macOS: label `com.deskree.obid`, home `~/Library/Application Support/Oby/Runner`, the This Mac home above) or a systemd user service (Linux: `obid.service`). With `--service`, `--prefix`, `--home`, `--config` and `--listen` it installs a second obid beside the first without touching it. The token is never in an argument, an environment variable, the service file or the configuration. docs/install.md also says how to update, uninstall and migrate from the Swift runner. **On a Mac, Oby.app runs obid itself** (2026-10-18): `Contents/Helpers/obid`, registered as the login item `com.deskree.oby.obid` with "This Mac", started as `obid serve` with no `--config` (macOS only: this configuration's file, `~/Library/Application Support/Oby/obid/config.json`, written the first time with `listen` 127.0.0.1:7777, this `home`, the login's name as `owner`, Claude Code and Codex when found, `gateway` 127.0.0.1:8810 and `telemetry` false; its log `~/Library/Logs/obid.log`), with `bundledPlugins` and `web` defaulting to the app's `Contents/Resources/obid/plugins` and `web`. An install.sh launch agent (`com.deskree.obid`) keeps running until the app moves it (docs/install.md).

### obid only: accounts

The owner adds, renames and removes the agents' accounts, and signs them in, from the apps (Settings › Accounts). The configuration's `agents.<tool>.accounts` are the seed. What these endpoints change is kept in `home/accounts.json` (0600) and laid over the seed at every start; the configuration file is never rewritten.

- **When a change applies.** Every change reaches routing, the brain and the gateway's scrubbing at once, and every app following `GET /v1/events` gets `accounts` (the whole list, as `GET /v1/status` has it).
- **Checks at start.** Each entry of `home/accounts.json` is checked as the API checks it, and one that isn't right is left out and logged: an id obid didn't make (eight lowercase hex digits: macOS ignores letter case, so two ids differing only in case would share a folder), a folder other than `home/accounts/<id>`, a name or payer the API would refuse, a name (added or changed) another account of the tool has, a change for an account obid doesn't have, or past 32 accounts.
- **A file that doesn't read.** obid logs why, runs with the configuration's accounts, and answers `503` to every change until the owner fixes or removes the file.
- **No tool comes back on its own.** A tool whose accounts were all removed is gone, rather than back on its own default login (the person's), which an empty `accounts` otherwise means. Its default login is listed, as `default` (Claude Code) or `codex` (Codex), once anything about the tool's accounts changed, and can be removed like any account.
- **Not on the gateway.** None of these endpoints is on the gateway's allowlist: a paired phone's token must never add an account or sign obid into someone else's login, which would send the owner's code to that account. The Mac app calls them over loopback.

**`POST /v1/accounts`** adds one:

```json
{"agent": "codex", "name": "Personal", "payer": "Wellington"}
```

- `agent` is `claude`, `codex` or `cursor`. A tool the configuration doesn't name gets one while it has an account, run as `claude`, `codex` or `cursor-agent` found on the login shell's `PATH`. A tool whose configured `accounts` is empty keeps its default login beside what's added.
- `name` and `payer` are each one line of 1 to 60 characters, trimmed. `payer` is optional: null or empty means none.
- No two accounts of a tool have the same name, nor a name that is another one's id, letter case aside: jobs name an account by either.
- obid makes the folder, `home/accounts/<id>` (0700), and removes it with the account. The API names no folder: a body with `configDir`, or any other key, is `422`. obid makes the `id` (eight hex digits). At most 32 accounts, the configuration's among them.
- `201` with the Account; `422` with the reason for a wrong body.

**`PATCH /v1/accounts/{id}`** with `{"name": "…"}`, `{"payer": "…" | null}` or both answers the Account. Errors are `422` as above, and `404` for an unknown account. The configuration's accounts can be renamed and given a payer too.

**`DELETE /v1/accounts/{id}`** answers `204`.

- A sign-in under way ends first; `409` (`Personal's sign-in didn't end within 5 seconds: try again in a moment.`) when it doesn't within 5 seconds.
- `409` while a job of the account is queued or running: `Personal has a job queued or running: stop it, or move it to another account, first.`
- Its folder is removed only when obid made it (a real folder, never through a link). A configuration account that is removed stays removed across restarts. Its id is never made again, and its folder (one obid didn't make stays) is still hidden from every box, kept in `home/accounts.json` (`retired`).
- What the tool itself keeps stays: on macOS, Claude Code's Keychain item for the folder (Keychain Access removes it), and the login on the provider's side (signed in with Anthropic, OpenAI or Cursor until the person signs it out there).

**`GET /v1/accounts/{id}/sign-in`** runs the tool's own login for the account, never obid's (AGENTS.md: obid never reads, stores or forwards the login), and answers what the person needs to finish it:

```json
{"account": "a1b2c3d4", "url": "https://claude.com/cai/oauth/authorize?code=true&…", "userCode": null,
 "pasteCode": true, "expiresAt": "2026-10-18T10:15:00Z"}
```

- **What the app does.** It opens `url` in the browser. `userCode` (Codex) is the one-time code the person types on that page. `pasteCode` (Claude Code) means the page ends by showing a code, which the app sends back with `POST`.
- **One at a time.** Asked again while it's under way, the answer is the same sign-in; there is one per account. It ends after 15 minutes (Codex's code lasts that long), or once it has signed in.
- **Jobs wait.** While a sign-in is under way, and while an account is being removed, no job is dispatched to the account (a job naming it is refused: `Personal is being signed in or removed: try again in a moment.`; routing passes it by) and none starts on it: a job queued on it in the meantime stays queued and starts once the change ends. That holds from the moment no job of it was queued or running, and both are looked at under the same lock. An abandoned sign-in is ended with `DELETE` (below), rather than keeping the account's jobs waiting until it runs out.
- **Where it runs.** The login runs in the account's own folder: no box ever gets that folder (#655, "The job sandbox"), so nothing a job wrote can steer the login. The tool writes the login there itself, and obid never reads or copies what it writes there except for the boxed runs' copies.
  - **Claude Code:** `claude auth login` with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` set to the account's folder. On macOS the login goes to that folder's Keychain item, the one obid copies for a boxed run with `claudeKeychain`, so on macOS it's `409` unless `claudeKeychain` is on. Without a terminal, Claude Code prints a link whose page shows the code, and reads the code on its stdin.
  - **Codex:** `codex login --device-auth -c cli_auth_credentials_store="file"` with `CODEX_HOME` and `HOME` set to the account's folder. It prints a link and a one-time code, and listens on no port (its browser login needs `localhost:1455`). It ends by itself once the person has signed in.
  - **Cursor:** `cursor-agent login` with `NO_OPEN_BROWSER=1`, the account's own `HOME`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` and `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`, and `AGENT_CLI_CREDENTIAL_STORE=file`, so its login is a file under the folder rather than the macOS Keychain (jobs run with the same setting and get a copy of that file). Its caches (`NODE_COMPILE_CACHE`, `XDG_CACHE_HOME`) go in obid's folder for the sign-in, and its `cli-config.json` is rewritten first, as before a job. It ends by itself once the person has signed in.
- **How it runs.** Every login runs unboxed, since it must reach the Keychain and the network:
  - by its absolute path (`503` when obid can't find one);
  - with its environment made from scratch: the kept variables, `PATH=/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin` plus the folder of the tool's own binary (links followed: Cursor runs the `node` beside it) and, for a script that starts `#!/usr/bin/env <program>` (a CLI installed with npm: `#!/usr/bin/env node`), the folder of that program as obid finds it on the login shell's `PATH`, no token, and no Claude Code or Anthropic variable;
  - with `BROWSER=true`, so nothing opens on the runner;
  - with `TMPDIR` and its working folder set to a new folder of obid's (`home/tmp/sign-in-…`, removed when it ends, and swept at obid's next start);
  - in a process group of its own, which obid kills when the login ends, when the sign-in runs out, is ended or the account is removed, when obid stops, and when the request that started it ends before the login gave its link (its folder removed with it).
- **What obid takes from its output.** What a login prints is read, at most 4,096 bytes of each line, only for the link and the code:
  - the link is the first `https://` link on the tool's own sites (`claude.com`, `claude.ai`, `anthropic.com`; `openai.com`, `chatgpt.com`; `cursor.com`, `cursor.sh`, or below them), with no login, port or space in it;
  - the code is Codex's, a line like `3U0G-DCVSU`.
  Nothing else it prints is kept, answered or logged. obid's log says only that a sign-in started and whether it signed the account in.
- **Errors.** `404` for an unknown account. `409` in four cases:
  - the tool's own default login (an account without a folder: it is the person's, signed in with the tool itself), and a folder whose login file or its lock (Claude Code's `.credentials.json…`, Codex's `auth.json…`, Cursor's `.cursor/auth.json` or `.config/cursor/auth.json` and the folders on the way) is a link or not the person's, since the login would write the new sign-in wherever it leads;
  - a job of the account is queued or running;
  - the account is being removed;
  - Claude Code on macOS without `claudeKeychain`.
  `502` when the login gave no link within 30 seconds.

**`POST /v1/accounts/{id}/sign-in`** with `{"code": "…"}` sends the code the page showed to Claude Code's login, on its stdin, once. The code is never kept or logged.

- It answers the Account once it's signed in.
- `422` when the code isn't one line of at most 4,096 characters, or when Claude Code didn't sign in with it (`Claude Code didn't sign in with that code. Start again for a new link.`: the sign-in has ended).
- `409` when no sign-in is under way, a code went to it already, or the tool takes no code (Codex, Cursor).
- `504` when it didn't end within 60 seconds.

**`DELETE /v1/accounts/{id}/sign-in`** ends the account's sign-in under way, with everything its login started: `204`, also when none was under way. `404` for an unknown account; `409` when it didn't end within 5 seconds.

When a sign-in ends, every app gets `accounts`, with `signedIn` as obid finds it (the login file, or the Keychain item with `claudeKeychain`).

### obid only: GitHub's sign-in

The owner signs obid's `gh` in to GitHub from the Mac (Settings › Connectors › GitHub, and the setup's GitHub step), with GitHub's own device sign-in; nobody types `gh auth login` (the owner's decision of 2026-10-18). obid never reads, holds or passes the token: gh keeps it in its own Keychain item, as `gh auth login` always does. None of these endpoints is on the gateway's allowlist: a paired phone never signs the Mac in to someone's GitHub.

**`GET /v1/github`** answers `{"gh": true, "signedIn": true, "user": "well-prado", "signIn": null}`:

- `gh`: whether obid has a GitHub CLI (the one Oby.app carries, else the login shell's).
- `signedIn` and `user`: the active github.com account in `gh auth status --hostname github.com --active --json hosts` whose `state` is `success` (never `--show-token`); signed out otherwise. `user` is a GitHub login name or null. Read again at most every 30 seconds, and after a sign-in ends.
- `signIn`: the sign-in under way, as `POST` answers it, or null.
